金融市場投資週報                 2026 年 10 月 05 日
上週重點訊息
◼ 美國製造業維持擴張，成本壓力升高：9 月 ISM 製造業調查顯示，美國製造業連續第九個月
位於擴張區間，新訂單與就業分項均見改善，反映接單與用人需求仍有支撐。然而，價格指數
由 8 月的 71.1 升至 9 月的 77.9，能源、金屬及關稅相關成本上升，加上供應商交貨偏慢，企
業面臨更大成本壓力 。 後續須觀察企業能否轉嫁成本 ， 以及通膨黏性對聯準會政策判斷的影響 。
◼ 日本短觀顯示製造業改善、服務業略緩：日本銀行 10 月 1 日公布的 9 月短觀顯示，大型製造

業景氣判斷指數由 6 月的＋22 升至＋24，大型非製造業則由＋37 降至＋35。企業預估 12 月
兩項指數分別回落至＋21 與＋30，顯示現況仍有支撐，後續看法則偏審慎。製造業改善有助
支撐日股獲利預期，但服務業放緩及日銀政策路徑仍須持續觀察。
◼ 台灣製造業活動加速擴張，AI 需求續強：10 月 1 日公布的製造業調查顯示，台灣 9 月製造業
活動擴張速度創 2021 年 8 月以來最快，AI 相關應用需求仍是重要推力。國發會 9 月 29 日公
布的 8 月景氣對策信號續呈紅燈，綜合判斷分數為 41 分，與 7 月持平，領先及同時指標皆續
升。科技供應鏈景氣仍有支撐，但成長動能能否擴及其他產業，仍是觀察後續景氣的重點。
市場展望
◼ AI 需求持續擴張，關注財報季表現： AI 基礎建設需求未受利率環境影響，而是持續擴張，帶
動光通訊、CCL、ABF 載板 、記憶體、先進製程與封裝等關鍵供應鏈維持強勁成長，市場表
現由全面上漲轉向結構性分化。隨新一代 AI 伺服器放量、CSP 資本支出維持高檔，具供給優

勢、定價能力及訂單能見度延伸至 2027 年的龍頭廠商仍可望受惠。10 月中旬起陸續公布的
第三季財報，若結果持續表現亮眼，可望為市場再添動能，屆時具 AI 供應鏈題材的台股與日
股亦可望受惠。
◼ 債券佈局聚焦 5~10 年期美國公債及投等債：雖然貨幣政策風險 下降有助於穩定長期通膨預
期，但政府與企業發債供給增加所帶來的排擠效應，以及美國經濟成長展現韌性，仍可能使長
天期利率面臨上行壓力。因此，在當前利率環境下，5~10 年期債券較能兼顧票息收益與存續
期間風險管理。投資級債方面，彭博美國投資級公司債指數利差約 80-90 個基點，雖然利差
偏窄，但高基準利率環境仍提供具吸引力的絕對收益水準，因此現階段5~10 年期美國公債及
投資級債的風險報酬結構相對較佳。

## Document 068: 260930_daiwa_delta

Source PDF: all report pdf/260930_daiwa_delta.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 7
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Taiwan Information Technology

What’s new: We provide our views on Delta after c.4 months of share price 
weakness, driven by investor concerns on HVDC delays, and a slowdown 
in power consumption trend amid deceleration in Nvidia’s GPU shipment 
growth YoY. We remain positive on Delta over 2026-28E, as rising AI ASIC 
server trend should drive a meaningful increase in DC-DC power module 
ASPs (vs. Nvidia’s discrete form factor). We expect higher HVDC adoption 
from 2028E, driven by IT rack and power supply architecture changes.

What’s the impact: Component shortages are the key near-term 
bottleneck. We attribute the recent share-price weakness to slower-than-
expected revenue momentum in both 2Q26, due to vendor-sanction issue 
at Delta Thailand, and 3Q26E, due to a worsening shortage of power 
semiconductors. We believe the 2Q26 revenue shortfall was a one-off 
event, though 3Q26E growth is likely to remain constrained by the power-
semi shortage. While supply capacity should improve, the supply-demand 
gap will likely persist for some time. That said, end-demand should remain 
sound. Given the continued improvement in revenue momentum, we still 
expect operating margin to reach a record-high of 18.3% in 3Q26.

Long-term growth drivers. We see Delta as a key beneficiary of the AI 
ASIC server trend. We were previously concerned that rising AI ASIC 
adoption could hurt Delta, as ASICs have lower thermal design power 
(TDP) than Nvidia GPUs, but now believe that Delta will benefit from rising 
content value of DC-DC power modules, as hyperscalers are more willing 
to spend on DC-DC modules (vs. discrete) despite higher initial cost given 
their superior energy efficiency. We forecast DC-DC power module revenue 
to reach c.TWD60bn in 2026E (vs. TWD9bn in 2025 and our prior estimate 
of c.TWD20bn for 2026E). This figure could increase further with rising 
ASIC usage in 2027. We also believe HVDC adoption rate could reach at 
least 50% in 2028E (vs. our prior expectation of 30%), as power supply 
architecture is becoming increasingly bulky (to accommodate aluminium 
capacitors inside power supply amid the super capacitor shortage). Limited 
space in IT racks would force customers to adopt more power racks and 
likely equip them with high voltage versions to improve efficiency.

What we recommend: We raise our 2026-28E EPS by 2-15% on higher 
HVDC contribution. We lift our 12M TP to TWD3,088 (from TWD2,988) 
based on a lower target PER of 49x (from 55x) to reflect market sentiment, 
(at the high-end of its past-3-year range of 11-50x) applied to our 1-year-
forward EPS. Key downside risk: lower-than-expected demand.

How we differ: Our 2028E earnings is 21% above the Bloomberg 
consensus, likely due to our more bullish assumption for HVDC adoption.

30 September 2026

Key beneficiary of the AI ASIC server trend

➢ Better-than-expected DC-DC AI server contribution from 2026E 
➢ HVDC adoption rate should be higher-than-expected in 2028E 
➢ Reaffirming our Buy (1) rating and raising 12M TP to TWD3,088

Company profile 
Founded in 1971, Delta Electronics has been the global leader in switching power supply solutions since 
2002 and DC brushless fans since 2006. Its business covers 3 key areas: power electronics (eg, power 
supplies, thermal management products and passive components), automation (eg, controllers, robot 
arms and building management systems), and infrastructure (eg, datacentre infrastructure, networking 
and EV charging).

ESG risks
Risks Management Analyst comments
G
Executive/board
quality 1
Delta's board has 7 non-independent and 5 independent directors with a term of three years.
The total number of directors and ratio of independent directors appear higher than most other
listed companies. Besides, according to Delta's CSR report, enhancing the board's functions is
one of the key goals for the company to achieve better corporate governance; also, the board
members' diversity and professionalism will be taken into consideration to ensure a good board
quality. Moreover, the company evaluates all the directors in the board using internal regulations
and an external independent institution each year and at least once every three years,
respectively. We believe Delta has implemented relatively more enhanced measure than most
listed firms to improve its board quality.
Capital management 2
During 2016-19, Delta's payout ratio was stable in the range of c.70%, but fell to 56% in 2020
and 53% in 2021. For 2020, it was mainly due to a sizeable non-cash re-valuation gain after the
acquisition of Delta Thailand (DET), while the company maintained more cash on hand to deal
with the uncertainties caused by COVID-19. However, the DPS growth from 2016 to 2021 was
limited, as Delta struggled with business diversification into new end-markets (eg, smartphone,
NEV). We believe that Delta's effort started to bear fruit in recent years after its NEV business
has gradually picked up. Overall, we see a stable dividend policy from Delta and expect its DPS
to continue to grow YoY in the future given that they declared a TWD11.6 DPS for 2025 which
equals a 50% payout ratio.
Related party &
transaction 1 Revenue from related parties stood at 12% of total 2025 revenue and purchases from relatedparties were at 11%. We see limited risk from related party transactions.
S Supply chainmanagement 1
Delta focuses on: 1) localised management, 2) supplier certification and risk management, and
3) supplier sustainability engagement as key initiatives under the scope of supply chain
management. In 2019, 144 new suppliers joined Delta's supply chain system. All of them passed
environmental and social evaluations, signed the declaration of "non-use of conflict materials",
and complied with Responsible Business Alliance's (BRA) Code of Conduct. Also, these vendors
had to implement and abide by Delta's supply chain CSR policy and code of conduct. To lower
ESG risks, Delta carries out RBA audits and monitors high-risk critical suppliers each year. The
suppliers that fail these audits have to provide detailed improvement plans in response to
identified issues. Besides, Delta has surveyed a total of 263 suppliers with potential risk and 63
suppliers with high risk of the total 1,331 suppliers, based on its CSR policy and code of conduct.
If these suppliers are flagged as high-risk and no concrete improvements are made, Delta may
consider terminating cooperation and transactions with them to improve the company's
engagement on supplier sustainability. Overall, we think Delta has a solid plan to improve its
supply chain management with specific KPIs, including: 1) achieving supplier improvement rate
of 80% in 2025, 2) reducing GHG emissions rate of critical suppliers by 10% in 2025, and 3)
improving water saving rate of critical suppliers by 12% in 2025.
S Data security 2
Delta's Information Security team created Delta's Information Security Policy in November 2013
and started providing educational training to global employees in 2014. Delta gradually
introduced the Information Security Management System (ISMS) in 2016 and obtained the ISO
27001 international certification in 2018. According to ISO27001, Delta has to conduct at least
one internal audit on information security and appoint an independent institution to conduct audit
externally. According to Delta, no major deficiencies have been found in recent years.

## Document 152: Advanced economies _ Resilience to shock after shock (2026-09-24)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Advanced economies  _ Resilience to shock after shock (2026-09-24).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-24
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

3
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
History tends to repeat itself in advanced economies. Once again, growth ultimately fell short of expectations by only a small
margin in the first half of 2026, despite the conflict in Iran. As early as 2025, the impact of tariffs was less severe than feared.
This is a sign that structural factors (notably AI, defence and electrification) are underpinning growth in these countries

and are expected to continue to do so in 2027. However, there are significant risks to the economic outlook, primarily of a
geopolitical nature, with a significant upside risk to inflation and interest rates. The other instance of history repeating itself
concerns a period that those under 20 cannot have experienced: the period leading up to the 2008 crisis, echoes of which can
be seen in terms of growth, inflation, the direction of monetary policy, and long-term interest rates.

Advanced economies are experiencing trends similar to those they
faced nearly 20 years ago:
- Growth remains resilient in 2025-26 despite shocks, as it did in 2006-
07, in the major advanced economies.
- Inflation is above central banks’ targets: as in 2006-07, this calls for a
tightening of monetary policy.
- In Europe and the United States, long-term interest rates have re -
turned to their 2006-07 levels (1996 in Japan): a challenging environ-

ment for public finances in a context where public debt ratios are signi-
ficantly higher than those seen 20 years ago.
THE GLOBAL ECONOMY IN THE MIDST OF A GROWTH CYCLE
We are already well into 2026, and the picture so far is clear: glo -
bal growth is proving resilient. Following a US tariff shock last year,
growth is once again proving, for the time being, to be significantly less
affected by the conflict in Iran than had been anticipated when it first

broke out. Initially, however, there were fears that this new shock would
hit growth even harder. As with the tariff hike in 2025, the impact was
measurable, particularly in terms of consumer confidence. But in both
2025 and 2026, growth has also shown a degree of resilience.
The figures for the second quarter bear this out, despite the sharp rise
in oil prices, the recessionary impact of which is not yet apparent.
According to our forecasts, growth was expected to reach or exceed 1%

in 2026 in the four major advanced economies (the Eurozone, Japan,
the United Kingdom and the United States, where it exceeds 2%) for
the second consecutive year. This had not happened (excluding the
post-COVID catch-up period) since 2006-07. Growth figures are more
moderate (particularly for demographic reasons), but the private sec -
tor is not (at present) showing the same excesses (at that time, the
credit boom had preceded the worst crisis since the 1930s); although

debt levels have recently risen in the US tech sector.
The driving force behind the current growth cycle is clear: artificial
intelligence (AI). It is fuelling global trade and business investment
in most countries. This is clearly the case in the United States, where
business investment is growing strongly as investment plans in the
US tech sector are implemented.
However, the world’s major economies are currently engaged in a
broader push to expand their capital goods investment. Alongside the

ongoing development of services—particularly digital services—we
have seen a rebound in industrial production in most countries since
early 2025. The leading sectors are primarily technology equipment,
machinery, aeronautics, defence and their essential inputs (energy and
metals). This trend is evident everywhere, including in Europe, and
is becoming entrenched over the long term, with an improvement in
business climate indicators, particularly new export orders. Growth is

therefore expected to strengthen further in 2027, particularly in the
Eurozone, where German investment plans are set to gather momen -
tum and whose spillover effects will have a greater impact on the eco-
nomy.
However, not all sectors are experiencing the same momentum. This
is particularly true for the sectors that depend on household consump-
tion, which has shown mixed trends. Our observation that consumer
spending is more buoyant in the United States than in Europe was

confirmed in 2026, and the outlook does not point to a change in this
ranking.
EDITORIAL
RESILIENCE TO SHOCK AFTER SHOCK

### Page 2

4
EcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
The bank
for a changing
world
While European and US households are experiencing more or less the
same energy price shock, inflation remains higher in the United States.
But this has not prevented consumer spending from keeping pace, pri-
marily because it is driven by the wealthiest households, who are less
sensitive to inflation than to the wealth effect. Furthermore, while the

savings rate remains high in the Eurozone, US households have been
drawing down their savings. This is also true of British households,
which explains why the UK has seen slightly stronger growth since
the beginning of the year. In Japan, government spending has partially
offset energy inflation (while other countries are doing so to a much
lesser extent than in 2022), which has supported consumption growth.
However, these buffers—reduced savings or fiscal support—have their

limits when shocks persist. The two major current conflicts (Iran and
Ukraine) are far from being resolved, and their impact is continuing.
This persistent geopolitical risk and its implications for inflation repre-
sent a significant headwind to the growth outlook for 2027.
HIGHER INFLATION CALLS FOR MONETARY TIGHTENING
Inflation is exceeding the target set by three of the four central banks
examined in this publication, which is another similarity (albeit with

different underlying causes) with the 2006-07 period. Japan is the ex -
ception, as government support measures for households are tempo -
rarily holding back inflation.
Apart from the Bank of Japan, which is engaged in a long-term norma-
lisation process, no central bank was expected to raise its key rates
in 2026. But they are now being prompted to do so for reasons that
vary from one region to another. The first is the conflict in Iran and
the resulting rise in oil prices, which triggered a rebound in energy

inflation from the second quarter onwards. However, since the conflict
was not resolved by the memorandum of understanding signed in June,
and given that the subsequent drop in oil prices proved short-lived,
inflation is expected to rise again. The rise in gas prices is expected to
exacerbate the impact, particularly in Europe, where inventories are
being replenished ahead of winter. Electricity prices are expected to
follow suit in most countries, but at a more moderate pace than in

2022. Indeed, gas is now used less to generate electricity than it was
at that time (thanks to the expansion of renewable energy), and to an
even lesser extent in France.
There is a significant risk that energy inflation will spread further to
other components of inflation in the future, and inflation expectations
could eventually reflect this. This is why the European Central Bank
has already raised its key interest rate twice (in June and September)

and, according to our scenario, will likely do so again in December. For
the same reason, we expect the Bank of England to raise its policy rate
in November. Although the cap on energy prices was maintained for
a long time, it has now been raised. As a result, inflation is expected
to rise between now and the end of the year. The aim will then be to
prevent second-round effects.
Inflation can also be a sign of (too much) economic strength. In the

United States, it has exceeded the 2% target since 2021. The main
argument in favour of a new cycle of rate hikes (following the one in
2022-2023) lies in the current resilience of growth and the improving
labour market. The Federal Reserve (Fed) must balance two equally
important objectives: maximum employment and price stability. Howe-
ver, unlike in the 4 th quarter of 2025, when the decline in job crea -
tion justified monetary easing (by 75 basis points), the current sharp

rebound in job creation completely reverses that analysis. According
to our forecasts, the unemployment rate is expected to decrease in
the coming months, which pushes back the prospect of a decline in
core inflation. This situation persists due to both supply constraints
(over which a central bank has no control) and excess demand (which
the Fed can, however, mitigate). Consequently, the Fed raised its key
rate in September and is expected to implement two further rate hikes

between now and January 2027.
In Japan, finally, while headline inflation has been brought down by
government support measures, core inflation remains significant.
Wage pressures are mounting against a backdrop of labour shortages.
The Bank of Japan is therefore being forced to accelerate the pace of its
monetary normalisation.
In all these regions, inflation is currently expected to peak in Q4 2026
or Q1 2027. It is against this backdrop of temporary inflation that rate

hikes could also come to an end by that time (excluding Japan). Howe-
ver, this assumes that once the energy shock—the course of which
remains uncertain—subsides, inflation will fall. The effects of El Niño,
which are still difficult to gauge at present, could take over. Inflation
would then remain above the central banks’ target for a longer period.
Under this scenario, rate hikes would go further than is currently en -
visaged.
THE RISE IN LONG- TERM INTEREST RATES: A FACTOR THAT WILL WEIGH

ON PUBLIC FINANCES
Monetary policies have also to offset the inflationary effects of fiscal
policy, particularly in the United States (where the budget deficit is
high and shows no signs of shrinking) and in Japan (where the new
government is pursuing pro-growth policies). In Europe, fiscal policy
is playing a much smaller role than it did in 2022. While the stimulus
measures implemented at that time did help reduce the inflation rate
in the short term (by nearly 2.5 pp in France, for example), by keeping

demand at a high level, they delayed disinflation and increased the
scale of the monetary tightening required.
The outbreak of the conflict in Iran acted as a catalyst: it triggered a
rise in long-term rates (which had been trending downwards at the
start of the year) to levels close to those of 2006-07. This rise is wides-
pread, but its causes vary. Inflation expectations appear to play a more
significant role in the Eurozone, whereas the rise in real interest rates

seems to be the main factor in the United States and Japan.

The bank
for a changing
world
Higher inflation and more resilient real growth than feared point in
the same direction: stronger than expected nominal growth that is li -
kely to be sustained. However, in the long term, high nominal growth
tends to drive up nominal interest rates (as it is a positive sign of more
profitable investment projects), in contrast to the problem of secular

stagnation raised in the 2010s (which combined low nominal growth
with low nominal interest rates).
The other issue is the high level of fiscal deficits (too high for a period
of growth), with various situations. In several countries, the public
debt-to-GDP ratio continues to rise. In the United States, deficit reduc-
tion does not appear to be a priority, to the extent that it is expected
to remain above 6% of GDP at the very least until the end of Donald

Trump’s term of office (with little hope that the mid-term elections will
significantly alter this trend). In the United Kingdom and France, fiscal
consolidation has begun, but it is being hampered by the simultaneous
increase in certain areas of expenditure (debt servicing, defence and
healthcare, due in particular to an ageing population), which is slowing
the pace of consolidation. In Germany, the implementation of invest -
ment plans is leading to a widening of the deficit. While this does not

pose a sustainability problem, it does result in an increase in annual
debt issuance. In Japan, the budget deficit is kept in check by interest
payments that remain low, as the rise in interest rates is recent and
the average maturity of the debt is high (10 years). However, expansio-
nary fiscal policy could pose further problems in terms of debt dyna -
mics if it were to continue through the end of the decade. In Italy and
Spain, fiscal consolidation has been sufficient to stabilise the public

debt-to-GDP ratio (and even to reduce it in Spain), but the high level of
Italy’s debt service it vulnerable to a rise in interest rates.
In all these countries, the fiscal arithmetic is simpler today than it
will be in 2030. Indeed, in most cases, only half of the debt was is -
sued after 2022 (that is, after the end of the zero-interest-rate period).
The debt service has so far only partially risen, except in the United
States, where most of the increase has already occurred due to a shor-

ter average debt maturity (6 years). This means that, for all countries
combined, the rate of GDP growth remains higher than the average
interest rate paid on public debt (or the effective interest rate).
By 2030, the effective rate will have risen and caught up with
GDP growth. As a result, in most cases, the debt service will have re -
turned to the level as a share of GDP seen in the early 2000s. In order to
stabilise public debt-to-GDP ratios, primary balances will need to have

improved. This will even require the generation of primary surpluses,
particularly in Italy (which is already achieving this), France and the
United Kingdom. This is a crucial shift that governments will need to
introduce as early as the 2027 budget; otherwise, they risk postponing
the stabilisation of their public debt-to-GDP ratio once again.
Article completed on 17 September 2026
Stéphane Colliac
stephane.colliac@bnpparibas.com

## Document 169: Allianz_2026-10-08_From legacy capital to smart bets Europe's path to a competitive auto industry_a5c640fb

Source PDF: all report pdf/Allianz_2026-10-08_From legacy capital to smart bets Europe's path to a competitive auto industry_a5c640fb.pdf
Report source: Allianz Trade
Date: 2026-10-08
Pages: 16
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1

In Summary

• Chinese brands will dominate  the Paris auto show  next week . They represent 50% of 
exhibitors versus just 23% in 2024, a visible sign of a deeper, structural shift already 
reshaping the market.  China holds 10% of European registrations, 16% in battery electric 
vehicles (BEV) and 26% in plug-in hybrid electric vehicle s (PHEV). This comes as Europe's 
auto market defies a sluggish growth outlook, up +5% YTD through August, suggesting 
structural change rather than a cyclical blip. Low -carbon demand leads the charge: BEV 
sales are up nearly +45% YTD, reaching parity with petrol-powered engine s (22%), amid 
energy volatility, EV cost compression and targeted subsidies for low incomes. 
• A deeper rift is opening between political ambition and industrial execution, and it risks 
setting a technology trap. Easing targets now breeds complacency precisely when the shift 
to low-carbon production needs to accelerate. Europe is poised to soften carbon penalties 
and cut the 2035 zero -emission target to 90%, relieving Original Equipment Manufacturers 
(OEMs) who underinvested rather than forcing them to catch up. Support was needed to 
share the burden, but without a binding roadmap, automakers will simply buy time, falling 
further behind Chinese rivals. Demand itself is uneven : BEVs are today the second biggest 
segment in France and Germany (30 and 26%), yet lag below 10% in Italy, Spain or even in 
the key but less accessible US market, exposing a dangerous short -term versus long -term 
arbitrage. 
• Added-value erosion of EUR3bn by 2030 must be urgently contained. The shift in dominant 
technology and rising competition from China have eroded the big three German carmakers' 
market share materially in core markets – down 10pps in Europe and 7pps in China since 
2021. In Europe, where manufacturing costs are less competitive, domestic added-value per 
vehicle in H1 2026 was 30-45% lower than in 2021/2022 while in China that decline has been 
smaller, less than 20% over the same period. A further realistic 3pps market share slide in 
both markets by 2030 would translate into a EUR3bn added-value deterioration for German 
OEMs, equivalent to 4pps of EBITDA margin. 
• Whoever secures reliable, cost-competitive battery production will shape the next decade 
of automotive competitiveness . Vehicle value is migrating from the factory floor to the 
software stack: Engine and chassis once represented 35 –45% of an internal combustion 
engine (ICE) car's value, but battery and software now account for 50 –60% of a BEV's. 
"Made-in-Europe" content requirements would preserve some manufacturing jobs, but at a 
steep cost: ~ EUR2,000 per vehicle (+5% retail price), coverage of only ~50% of domestic 
battery demand by 2035 and a wider technology gap with global peers. They would do little 
to reduce sovereign risk either: over 70% of European-based battery technology will be held 
by Asian firms , according to the current pipeline . The more achievable path is to grow 
Europe's footprint in capital -light, high -value segments (design and IP ), capturing added 
value without the capital risk of manufacturing.  
• Europe should pursue smart partnerships over full sovereignty, anchoring Asian battery 
and chip production on European soil, pooling procurement and R&D and consolidating 
onto fewer EV platforms under strict capital discipline. Diversifying further into  into 
defense looks relevant it has strong public funding but is running out of capacity . The long-
term bets worth making are solid-state batteries, recycling, proprietary chip design, local AI 
infrastructure and autonomous trucking and logistics, where Europe can differentiate 
without competing head on against entrenched US and Chinese leaders.

Guillaume Dejean  
Senior Sector Adv isor

From legacy capital to smart bets : Europe's path to 
a competitive auto industry

### Page 2

2

A legacy capital mindset behind a structural crisis 
The 2026 Paris auto show  is emblematic of the changing global order  in the automotive industry . European 
brands account for just 40% of exhibitors in this year’s edition of the flagship industry event , to be held from 12-18 
October, down from 68% compared to 2022 and 2024. This pullback reflects intense cost-cutting measures amid a 
severe profitability crisis, with manufacturers engaging in tactical arbitrage. Meanwhile, Chinese players are seizing 
the opportunity and confirming their strong interest in the European market: Chinese brands surged from 9% to 50% 
of exhibitors at the event, and Chinese OEMs from 15% to a dominant 63% by 2026, using this prime European stage 
to attract new audiences and accelerate their continental expansion. This highlights the rebalancing of power within 
the automotive industry, where dynamic and innovative new competitors are openly contesting the long -held 
dominance of Europe's established giants. While this is  not the industry's first downturn, this time the weakness is 
structural rather than cyclical, and its root is identifiable: a legacy capital mindset that, for two decades, funded the 
defense of mature ICE assets and short -term margins instead of the electric, digital and industrial bets that would 
determine the next cycle. The technology lag, the China shock and the brand erosion are best read not as separate 
failures but as symptoms of that same underlying capital allocation culture.

Figures 1 & 2 : Breakdown of attendees of Paris auto show by  origin of OEMS and brands  /  
12-month trailing revenue, profits and margin rate from largest German automakers

Sources: Salon de l’Auto notice, LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

A legacy capital mindset: capital defended the past, chased the quarter and bet small when it finally moved.  
European automakers' balance sheets tell the story of a capital culture built for a different era. For years, capital 
was steered to sustain and optimize mature ICE platforms and defend near -term margins – the sunk-cost logic of 
an incumbent reluctant to cannibalize its own cash cow – rather than to pre-fund the electric and digital transition 
while there was still runway to do it gradually. When bets were finally placed, they were sized and governed like 
legacy capex, not like venture-style industrial transitions: The ill-fated decision to internalize middleware software 
development, a domain far removed from automakers' core competencies, burned time and money for negligible 
results, while billions in diesel -related penalties and surging impairment charges drained c ash reserves that could 
instead have funded the transition of ICE plants to EV production lines, scaled battery gigafactories or fueled critical 
R&D. Historically, the auto sector led capex growth during expansion years but proved more vulnerable in 
downturns. Yet today's situation is uniquely alarming: The broader economic cycle remains steady while auto capex 
is collapsing ( -20%, its strongest contraction since 2018) even as non -auto European blue-chip capex expands by 
+10%. That is short-termism showing up directly in the numbers: capital retreating precisely when software-defined 
vehicles, AI -powered autonomous systems and the early diffusion of robotaxis are reshaping the industry's  
competitive frontier. Having already missed the EV wave and fallen behind in the software revolution, European 
carmakers cannot afford to sit out the autonomous race. Yet the pause on Level 3 development is another example 
of risk -averse, low -conviction capital deployment at exactly the wrong moment. In this industry, moats and

technology edges erode fast. Today's investments are tomorrow's profits, and a mindset built to defend yesterday's 
assets has left financial leeway too thin to allow another misjudgment. 
Figure 3 : Evolution of capex from European automakers vs. Stoxx 600 benchmark (annual growth & share)

Sources:  LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research  
The second structural weakness is a  late EV transition.  European OEMs didn't just misjudge the pace of 
electrification, they kept capital deployed to defend ICE production and margins for as long as the balance sheet 
allowed, leaving too little invested when the pivot could no longer be delayed. Electrification diffused faster than 
they bet on: BEVs now account for over 20% of EU registrations (on par with ICE this year) and nearly 50% in China, 
the fastest-growing segment on both continents, and European carmakers are scrambling to catch up. The late 
pivot from ICE to EV and hybrid production is now compressing costs into a brutal timeframe: what could have been 
a gradua l, capital -light transition is instead an expensive, chaotic restructuring. German OEM margins have 
collapsed from 13% in 2022 to roughly 4% today. BEV profitability remains structurally lower than ICE, while Chinese 
competitors deliver advanced technology  at aggressive price points. The same mindset shows up in how setbacks 
were funded: Diesel-gate alone cost Volkswagen over USD34bn, capital that was unavailable to fund a decade of 
electrification R&D instead. The traditional European advantage  – a deep, local supplier ecosystem built on 
mechanical engineering – is eroding fast as EV supply chains shift toward Asian battery and component makers. 
European OEMs may have caught up on sales volumes at home, but their cost structures remain bloated, their 
margins thin and their competitive moat narrower by the quarter.

registration in EU-27 vs. margin rate and free cash flow yield from top German OEMs

The third structural weakness is competition from  China: the price of capital that protected margins instead of 
market share.  Over the past decade, China was the biggest and most profitable market for German OEMs, 
accounting for over 30% of group revenue , and capital allocation treated that profit pool as something to protect 
rather than a market worth fighting for with scale. That era is over as the balance of power has been fully disrupted 
by the rise of local players that quickly absorbed foreign brands' market share. The miscalculation was strategic as 
much as financial: European OEMs underestimated Beijing's electrification ambition and the speed at which 
domestic, and in some cases less-experienced, firms would scale, while committing far less capital to matching that 
scale than the threat warranted. Competition has been exported over the past years, with Chinese brands 
redirecting overcapacity mainly towards Europe as high tariffs have almost entirely closed access to the US market. 
Their EU market share surged from about 2% to nearly 10% by mid -2026, totaling over 1mn cars sold on the 
continent for the first time. EU tariffs of up to 35% barely slowed them: Chinese OEMs pivoted to hybrids and PHEVs, 
offered advanced tech at aggressive prices and adopted European -style design. The trade picture tells the rest. 
Europe's historical automotive surplus with China, once peaking near EUR20bn, has flipped into an unprecedented 
deficit. EU auto exports to China halved in two years. In the opposite direction, Chinese EV and hybrid exports to the 
EU doubled to USD14.2bn in H1 2026 alone, with hybrid shipments surging +206%. The deficit is no longer just about 
BEVs; it spans hybrids, PHEVs and components, reflecting the broader penetration of Chinese brands while the 
European footprint in China fades. There is no reason to believe this wave will recede in the near future as OEMs 
intensify relocation of some production capacities in Europe to dodge any trade barrier threat. In the meantime, 
much improvement has been mad e on the after-sales networks and client relationships that remained one of the 
last Achilles' heels of Chinese firms in Europe, and the value proposition is perfectly adapted to European customer 
demand: a good -looking and affordable advanced technology which is precisely the segment European capital 
under-invested in. 
Figures 6 & 7 : Share of imported Chinese brands in Europe vs. exported European brands in China / trade balance 
between EU-27 and China per powertrain breakdown (in EUR bn)

Sources:  Eurostat, CAAM, Allianz Research  
The fourth structural weakness:  Brand power fades once the capital behind the premium stops compounding.  
The aura of German automotive excellence, built on decades of engineering prestige and glossy design , was itself 
a form of capital, accumulated through sustained investment in mechanical engineering. It is cracking now that the 
industry pivots toward electrification and digitalization. The core value in a 2026 vehicle now sits in software-defined 
solutions and battery systems, both firmly controlled by Asian firms, leaving European carmakers defending a 
premium price tag no longer backed by technological superiority. Competitors, notably Chinese brands, deliver a 
sharper performance-to-price ratio, while consumer price sensitivity has surged since the 2022 energy crisis as car -
related costs (i.e. insurance, energy and maintenance) skyrocketed by 20-30% over the core inflation rate since 2020. 
The 2026 EV boom cannot be decoupled from volatile energy prices and aggressive price compression driven by 
fiercer competition and widening supply. This mirrors the broader luxury backlash: heritage alone no longer justifies 
the invoice when the capital that built it was never reinvested in the technology that now defines the category. 
Meanwhile, Europe's most popular segment (i.e. sedans and hatchbacks below EUR50,000) carries a 3 to 5% better 
price-to-range ratio than Chinese BEV models currently sold in Europe yet low export value as different consumer 
tastes and cheaper local alternatives in emerging markets cap revenue upside.  Brand awareness, once the

industry's most durable moat and the clearest return on decades of engineering capital, fades into irrelevance as

Not a premium purchase anymor e? EV adoption is increasingly breaking the psychological glass ceiling that has 
long confined electric cars to an affluent, early -adopter customer base.  The shift from broad -based subsidies 
towards targeted support is making EVs more accessible to mainstream households, while falling vehicle prices and 
improving total cost of ownership are progressively chasing away the perception of EVs as a premium goods. This 
virtuous circle is particularly important for the fleet market: As companies increasingly electrify their fleets, a 
growing volume of relatively young EVs will enter the second -hand market, expanding choice and bringing prices 
within reach of a much broader consumer base. This lifts one of the main structural barriers to adoption so far: the 
limited maturity of the used-EV market and the sharp depreciation suffered by first-generation models, which made 
residual values difficult to predict and leasing costs comparatively high. Energy-price volatility is another factor that 
adds up this year and strengthens the economic case.

Four industrial equations and the capital choices that solve them 
Solving the battery puzzle 
A must -have to support ongoing fleet electrification. As the automotive industry undergoes its most profound 
transformation in a century, batteries have become the undisputed centerpiece of the electric vehicle. Representing 
30–35% of a BEV's total value, no other single component carries as much influence on vehicle cost, range, 
performance and, ultimately, consumer adoption. This makes battery supply not just a procurement question but a 
strategic one – for OEMs, for governments and for Europe's industrial base at large. With BEV penetration rates 
climbing steadily to come close to a 100% share by 2035, the volume of cells required is set to grow exponentially. 
Whoever secures reliable, cost-competitive access to battery production will shape the next decade of automotive 
competitiveness. For Europe, carmakers cannot continue depending  on external suppliers for such a critical 
components. This is one of the keys to solving the profitability gap. 
The added-value of European-sold vehicles has depreciated by 30-45%  since 2021/2022 period. Looking at the 
top three German carmakers’ balance sheets and the annual evolution of car sales per market over the past years, 
we find that added-value per vehicle in H1 2026 declined by around -30% and -45% Europe since 2021/2022 period, 
while in China current added -value declined by -20% since the 2022 peak but is today almost equivalent to 2021 
level. Over this period we can observe a sharp decline of German OEM market share by 10% in Europe and 7% in 
China as the technology shift and consumer demand change reframe the market structure. Based on our 
calculations, we then estima te that a market share decline  of 50bp s in China and Europe , which would mean an 
annual deterioration of added-value by EUR480mn. A realistic continuous slide based on actual momentum by -3% 
market share in each market by 2030 would then account for a loss of around EUR3bn of added -value, or 4% of 
annualized 2026 EBITDA.

### Page 7

Self-autonomous production is not for today but can be considered tomorrow. The bloc has built enough pack -
assembly capacity to match domestic BEV and PHEV demand, yet production remains overwhelmingly foreign -
controlled: South Korean manufacturers alone operate roughly 65% of installed capacity, concentrated in heavier, 
shorter-lived nickel-ion chemistry (NMC). However, European production is less competitive compared to  Asian 
peers, and notably China. Due to a  poor resource base in lithium, cobalt and rare earths, a drastic labor cost 
differential (roughly 1 per 5) and initially weak domestic demand, European-branded cell production was recording 
thin-razor margins. Several projects collapsed under scale constraints even as order books were full. On this basis, 
there was not much choice  other than imports of foreign technology, hence a widening trade deficit in a strategic 
inputs that has sharply intensified over the past quarter as BEV demand in Europe increased massively. On 
batteries, the EU recorded a record high deficit last year of EUR47bn, which should be broken again this year based 
on current trends (EUR57bn as of July on an annualized basis). 
Chasing full supply-chain autonomy now is the wrong fight: Europe arrived too late and has too little runway to 
out-scale Chinese lithium-ion economics. Policy should pivot from building a sovereign chain to securing one, using 
instruments like the EU's localization rules (IAA -style content requirements) to anchor foreign capital and 
technology on European soil. Importing South Korean and, selectively, Chinese lithium -ion know -how is not 
capitulation; it preserves jobs, de -risks logistics and buys time, while accepting that near -term competitiveness in 
conventional cells is structurally out of reach.  It seems to be the path picked by European automakers as we have 
seen over the past month a multiplication of greenfield investment into European battery capacity from Chinese 
firms. Based on current public announcements, China’s pipeline expects an increase of capacity of roughly 150 GW 
by 2030, multiplying the country’s market share by more than three from 15% to 37% (and even 45% when including 
no-committed options).  
Europe should not confound industry support with sovereignty protection . On 7 October, Germany and France 
agreed to push for a further easing of the EU's 2035 car CO₂ targets – seeking an 80% reduction rather than the 
Commission's already softened 90% proposal – in exchange for "Buy European" local -content requirements on 
batteries sold in Europe. This deal comes alongside the EU's new EUR1.5bn Battery Booster Facility, designed to 
help European cell manufacturers ramp up production. Yet the two measures risk working at cross -purposes. 
Relaxing emission standards would inevitably slow EV adoption, shrinking the very demand that European battery 
makers need to achieve the scale economies required to compete. Europe's battery landscape remains 
overwhelmingly dominated by Asian players: current greenfield investment pipelines suggest South Korean and 
Chinese suppliers will command at least 70% of European market share by 2030 –2035, leaving homegrown firms 
with a modest 15–20%. By diluting the regulatory signal that underpins EV uptake, the Franco-German compromise 
may shield legacy automotiv e jobs in the short term, but it risks starving Europe's fledgling battery industry of the 
volumes essential to close the cost gap with entrenched Asian competitors – ultimately deepening, rather than 
reducing, the continent's technological dependence.

### Page 8

Regulation is a necessary defensive step but not the ultimate solution . The economics are blunt: we estimate 
Made-in-Europe content requirements add roughly EUR2,000, or about a 5% mark-up, to a battery pack's final price 
– a premium consumers absorb at precisely the moment BEV-ICE cost parity is meant to narrow, and when energy-
price volatility and tighter credit are already denting appetite for large purchases. The passing-through strategy is 
not timely and could be unfruitful as it could grip the scale-up pattern on demand. A quick look at domestic battery 
capacity pipeline in Europe back s up our point. Even under the most optimistic scenario (i.e. every announced and 
pipeline project realized, every factory running at full nameplate utilization ), European cell capacity will not cover 
all retail demand. In a more realistic approach – a path toward a BEV share at or near 100% by 2035 – we calculate 
that European capacity would cover barely half of retail demand by 2035. That is meaningful progress, but it is not 
self-sufficiency, and it leaves no realistic path to excluding Chinese technology without rationing consumer choice 
or inviting retaliation. The honest conclusion: sovereignty is worth pursuing, but only paced to what capacity actually 
allows. Europe cannot build from zero to one hundred, and has no time to try. The better bet is to use today's 
dependence as a bridge by protecting jobs and the ecosystem while demand scales and direct policy not at 
refighting yesterday's lithium -ion battle, but at owning the chemistries, solid -state and beyond that will define the 
next one.

Not doomsday, but the path forward is narrow.  Europe cannot afford to treat batteries as a secondary concern: 
EV traction is set to intensify under current mandates, and carbon-neutrality commitments are too consequential to 
let such a critical technology layer remain overwhelmingly dependent on foreign supply. But the response has to be 
realistic. Weaker cash reserves, thinner market support and tightening lending conditions are real investment 
constraints for European OEMs, and attempting to build a fully sovereign, vertically integrated battery industry from 
scratch is neither financially viable nor strategically sound, especially when domestic players cannot yet match the 
cost or technology benchmarks set by Asian champions; carrying that burden alone would divert capital from areas 
where Europe can genuinely differentiate. A EUR1bn battery factory inaugurated in Germany  in early October 
shows what a realistic middle path looks like: While cells continue to be sourced from China, the factory's true added 
value lies in its operational verticality (i.e. a full -stack deployment spanning R&D, design, battery pack 
manufacturing and recycling ). It demonstrates how supply -chain dependencies can be mitigated, even if fully 
eliminating them remains realistically unachievable.

### Page 9

In the short run, the sector should embrace smart partnerships, and turn recycling into a domestic feedstock.  
Strategic alliances with leading South Korean and Chinese cell manufacturers should be actively pursued but 
provided production is anchored on European soil to secure local employment, build industrial know -how and 
reduce supply-chain disruption risks . This should be alongside a pan -European procurement or spending -pooling 
mechanism that consolidates bargaining power and lowers unit costs without resorting to protectionism. In parallel, 
battery recycling should be scaled into a genuine domestic feedstock: recovery of lithium, cobalt and nickel from 
end-of-life packs is costly today, but as cell volumes scale with BEV penetration, unit economics should improve 
sharply, reducing reliance on imported raw inputs without requiring Europe to out-build Asian cell economics. 
In the longer run, bet on solid-state, the next chemistry generation. China's lead there remains unsettled even as 
it targets the technology in its latest five-year plan. Lithium is finite while Europe holds meaningful sodium 
reserves, and performance-per-lifespan economics should eventually undercut today's chemistries. This is the one 
battery front where Europe can compete on a reset map rather than catch up on an existing one. 
Balance the software vs hardware  
From engine to code line, the control center has shifted away. Europe has no choice but to take into consideration 
the new paradigm that is expected to intensify under a broader adoption of AI -empowered solutions and 
accelerated development of autonomous mobility tools. Though the leaders of automotive chips are mostly located 
in Europe, European-made production dramatically decline d in 40 years, down from 20% to hardly more than 5% 
this year. And Europe does not produce or design the leading -edge chips used for AI development (frontier model, 
robotics, software). The sharp decline in revenue and margins (slashed by 2.5x from its 2023 peak of over 20%) of 
Europe’s leading auto chipmakers is as much evidence of weak European industrial demand as of late deployment 
of domestic production supporting both AI -powered and autonomous mobility solutions. Today, European 
carmakers prefer using US technology and hardware due to their high -performance computing capacities. With 
Level 2+ autonomous features already accounting for roughly a third of new registrations this year, and forecast to 
surpass 70% by 2035, it is an addressable market of almost between 585 -650mn of vehicles over the 2026 -2035 
period.  If Europe's chip champions remain anchored to legacy power and analog niches while the car becomes a 
rolling data center, the continent risks permanent relegation in the technology race that will define the next decade 
of mobility.

Europe's software blind spot: the hidden dependency behind next -generation autonomous cars . Even the most 
advanced European car models , featuring L2+ driving assistance, AI -powered cockpits and cloud -connected 
services, rest on a technology stack overwhelmingly controlled by non -European players. As the architecture map 
of a leading European vehicle platform reveals, the critical layers , from semiconductors to AI assistants to cloud & 
data storage and mapping are sourced almost entirely from US and, for local market variants, Chinese providers. 
This is a structural exposure. Semiconductors represent the sharpest vulnerability, where Europe faces a persistent 
technology gap with US leaders like NVIDIA and Qualcomm, and no credible short -term substitute exists at the 
performance frontier required for autonomous driving  compute. Yet the picture is not entirely bleak. European 
OEMs have strategically retained in -house control over the operating system ensuring sovereignty over the core 
software layer that orchestrates all other functions. Co -development models on driving assistance stacks allow 
European players to maintain architectural oversight and avoid full dependency on licensed black-box solutions. IT 
infrastructure is similarly co-owned rather than fully externalized. On AI assistance, emerging European alternatives 
such as Mistral offer a credible path to reduce reliance on US hyperscalers. The imperative is clear: Europe cannot 
eliminate its dependency overnight, but it can , and must , deepen internal OS mastery, expand co -development 
leverage, nurture homegrown AI capabilities and treat semiconductor sovereignty as a decade -long strategic 
investment rather than a problem to be solved quarterly. 
Figure 17 : Current software and hardware infrastructure of most-advanced European L2+ car model

Sources:  Corporate public information, Allianz Research  
Software as a driver of revenue migration from mechanical to data -centered operations. The end -to-end car 
software market is set to grow from roughly USD340bn in 2025 to USD520bn by 2035, a sustained +10% CAGR, with 
the fastest growth concentrated in AI -driven and automation segments (i.e. software verification and validation 
services and software development itself ), outpacing hardware segments like control units  or sensors. The value 
chain is migrating from mechanical engineering to code, and with it comes a new revenue layer already visible in 
the market: over -the-air conduct and performance  features (heated seats, horsepower monitoring, acceleration 
boost), advanced automated driving assistance  (FSD) and usage -based insurance and predictive -maintenance 
services built on vehicle telemetry. These models convert a one -off hardware sale into recurring, higher -margin 
revenue, improving cash -flow visibility well beyond unit sales.  The diversification approach made by the US 
manufacturer Tesla could be an example as the services segment accounted for 16% of its total revenue (vs. 6% in 
2022) and offset a part of the decline of cash flow generated by the direct sales of cars that account for roughly 70% 
of its revenue against 85% at its peak in 2022.

Connected service value is locked up into recurrence : a 8% additional revenue share to not overlook . A simple 
simulation illustrates the point: assuming 1 mn vehicles sold per year over a decade, a EUR50/month subscription 
plan with consumer adoption gradually rising from 33% to 50%, cumulative recurring revenue reaches 
approximately EUR3bn by year 10 , translating into a roughly 70% CAGR from a modest ~EUR200mn base in year 
one. In context, that terminal run -rate would represent around 8% of the average 2025 annual revenue of top 
European carmakers, making it a meaningful diversification channel rather than a rounding error. Crucially, 
software-driven services carry structurally higher margins than vehicle manufacturing, and the EUR50 price point is 
conservative, as advanced features such as autonomous driving upgrades, predictive maintenance or in-car digital 
ecosystems mature, willingness to pay should rise in tandem. Once retention loops are proven and the service 
portfolio deepens, this segment has the potential to evolve from a supplementary income stream into a core profit 
engine as the business model is fully optimized. 
In the short run, firms should  secure a foothold in assembly, and stop duplicating R&D.  Full onshoring is neither 
realistic nor necessary: Building domestic chip assembly and packaging capacity , even on licensed foreign 
technology, as the battery segment demonstrates , is vastly preferable to total dependence on Asian production, 
provided a recalibrated public -private financing mix and faster infrastructure permitting remove the region's self -
inflicted red-tape deterrent. The same logic applies to R&D: high competition should not preclude partnership in 
non-core, pre-competitive domains. VW's Cariad experience , billions spent with limited scale , is a cautionary tale 
worth internalizing; Pooling R&D capital across OEMs to explore frontier technologies where no single player holds 
a decisive lead, then differentiating at the product and service layer, is a model already emerging in Japan that 
Europe should engage with, provided governance structures ensure ecosystem-wide benefit. 
Going forward, European automakers should own the chip IP, and treat software as the real upside. Europe won't 
close the semiconductor manufacturing gap, but it doesn't need to: Much like the GPU/CPU ecosystem where value 
accrues to designers rather than fabs alone, Europe should channel its engineering depth and thriving SME -AI 
ecosystem into proprietary, industry -specific chip design, particularly for next -generation autonomous vehic le 
architectures as the intellectual property moat matters more than the production line. The same shift in where value 
sits applies to software: the revenue and margin potential of data -driven services and physical -AI applications in 
vehicles is still largely unsized, yet t he digitalization of cars is an irreversible trend. Capturing that value requires 
reducing strategic dependence on US and Chinese hyperscalers, prioritizing local cloud and AI infrastructure would 
unlock economic returns while materially lowering disruption risk.

Getting ready for autonomous mobility 
Robotaxis are arriving in Europe , but r egulatory gates are opening gradually . Berlin's autonomous bus pilot 
alone spans a 2023 -2027 runway from planning to launch, with authorization landing only mid -2027, while China 
and the US have already built a 100+ city blueprint to borrow from. The bigger brake may be cultural: European 
approval of autonomous vehicles sits near 33%, against roughly 60% in China. That gap is narrowing, but slowly, 
and 2027 -2028 will s tay experimental rather than commercial . Even generously scaled, Europe's robotaxi fleet 
reaches only 300-600k vehicles by 2035, half the US, a third of China's, and capturing at best 17% of global volume 
in a bull case, 7% in base. Europe's approval architecture is consumer -protection-first, business-case second, and it 
shows in the timeline. Each extra approval layer compresses the payback window on capex -heavy hardware, and 
that asymmetry is precisely what deters a European manufacturing play: no domestic tech champion exists, entry 
costs are steep and the regulatory calendar guarantees Europe will always scale second.

Robotaxis are the headline, but not the biggest or the smartest door into autonomy. Autonomous trucking is set 
to scale faster and bigger than robotaxis , a projected five-year CAGR of roughly +85% versus +50% for robotaxi s, 
with the truck segment reaching close to USD550bn by 2035 against robotaxi's USD420bn, while L3/L4 technology 
is still expected to represent under 9% of new car registrations that same year. That's a small, capital -intensive, 
already-concentrated market: China and the US dominate deployment  and the upfront fleet, and software costs 
are steep. A European OEM entering now would be underwriting high risk for thin, late-mover reward. The smarter 
play is backing adoption, not owning it , by partnering with established tech providers to secure distribution rights 
and data access across a market that will fragment into multiple channels (freight, transit, passenger) rather than 
building proprietary stacks from scratch. Robotaxis ’ real value to Europe are as a technology bridge: the same 
sensing and routing layers proven there translate directly into autonomous public transport, which aligns far better

with the continent's own agenda (i.e. decarbonizing cities, easing urban congestion and extending mobility services 
to an aging population etc.). Partnership is how Europe can capture the technology without paying the first-mover

Figure 22 & 23: Estimated global market size of robotaxis and autonomous trucks (in USD bn) / estimated market

Firms should  invest for the next decade, not the next earnings call.  Short-termism is the enemy of disruptive 
technology: Autonomous mobility demands heavy upfront capital with payback horizons that don't fit a quarterly 
mindset. The temptation is to chase whichever segment looks hottest today and retreat at the first cost overrun , 
exactly the pattern that made Cariad expensive and robotaxi economics forbidding. The discipline needed is the 
opposite: identify which autonomous layers compound in value over 10 years, commit capital in stages tied to proof 
points and resist the urge to fund flashy pilots that don't scale into durable operational channels. 
A tactical and timely shift toward defense 
Defense diversification is the elephant in the piece, but not yet plainly assumed by carmakers.  Defense 
diversification remains the unspoken opportunity for European carmakers , but the window to act is narrowing. As 
EU defense budgets surpass 2% of GDP, with frontrunners like Poland exceeding 4%, automakers are quietly 
exploring adjacencies in the defense sector. The industrial logic is compelling: Advanced mechanical engineering, 
scalable manufacturing capacity, and overlapping supply chains position them naturally to address surging 
demand for unarmored vehicles, armored platforms and UAV components . These are precisely  categories where 
volumes have accelerated sharply since the onset of the Russia-Ukraine conflict. Historically, European automakers 
have pivoted to defense production during periods of geopolitical stress, making this convergence neither new nor 
far-fetched. The timing is uniquely favorable: defense primes face capacity bottlenecks despite record backlogs and 
are integrating AI -driven autonomous systems, while automakers urgently need to absorb excess capacity and 
amortize R&D in autonomous driving. On paper, this is a textbook win-win. Yet tangible P&L impact remains absent. 
Carmakers should move beyond exploratory postures , through joint ventures, dedicated defense units or co -
development programs in order  to capture a share of the unprecedented public funding flowing into European 
defense. This is not about abandoning core business; it is about extending industrial horizons where growth is most 
visible today.

Capital discipline and capacity management are key 
The constraint is behavioral, not financial.  Auto capex is collapsing ( -20%, the sharpest contraction since 2018) 
even as non-auto European blue-chip capex expands by +10%, and European auto assembly plants ran near only 
60% utilization in 2023, a fixed-cost drag with no revenue offset. The sector isn't short of cash or ambition, as Cariad, 
battery megafactories  and robotaxi flirtations all show; it is short of a filter for where that capital and capacity 
should go. Closing this equation means fixing the allocation process itself, not raising more money. 
Cut platform complexity, and make discipline the filter.  European automakers are still running bloated multi -
platform ICE/hybrid/EV lineups that dilute scale economies on all three; with BEVs on track to dominate European 
registrations within the decade, the rational move is to consolidate platforms now rather than stagger the transition. 
Fewer, better-scaled EV architectures beat a wide portfolio hedging against a shift that's already underway. That 
same discipline needs to extend to capital allocation more broadly: harder capital-allocation frameworks, with kill-
criteria on projects before launch, smaller bets sized to realistic market share rather than blue -sky TAM and a bias 
toward partnership over proprietary build where the technology isn't core (software OS, autonomous stacks, battery 
chemistry).  
Stop absorbing idle capacity, redeploy it into defense.  Rather than mothball or subsidize underutilized plants, 
diversify output toward adjacent, high -demand sectors . Defense is the clearest fit, given sustained European 
rearmament budgets and long -dated procurement commitments. Precision manufacturing, electronics and 
systems-integration skills transfer directly, turning spare capacity into a second, less cyclical revenue stream rather 
than dead weight on the balance sheet.

Taiwan Industrials

Investment case: We initiate coverage of Acter, an established 
multinational cleanroom engineering, procurement, construction and 
management (EPCM) turnkey service provider, with a Buy (1) rating. Its 
core business includes cleanroom electromechanical engineering, 
water/gas/chemical delivery systems, and other cleanroom services. We 
believe now is a good time to revisit the stock as it underperformed the 
TAIEX Index by 32.7% in 3Q26, mainly on weaker-than-expected 2Q26 
results and soft sector sentiment. Given its stronger revenue momentum 
over recent months and upcoming TSMC results on 15 October, which may 
drive sentiment, we suggest investors to buy the stock. We forecast a 
24.6% revenue CAGR and 36.9% net profit CAGR over 2026-28E.

Intact global demand for cleanroom technology, the edge AI market, 
and rapid cleanroom expansion in Asia further support Acter’s order 
backlog. Its backlog reached TWD66bn in July 2026 vs. TWD46bn in 2025 
and TWD30bn in 2022 (15.3% 2022-25 CAGR), which largely outpaced the 
global cleanroom technology market’s forecast CAGR over 2023-2032E, 
according to Precedence Research.

Strong competitive edge supports order share gains across multiple 
segments. Acter’s competitive edge lies in 1) intelligent management 
enabled by digital twins and AI, using 3D building information modelling 
(BIM) and AI-driven digital twin management; 2) intellectualisation of green 
engineering, which comprises green design, green procurement, and green 
techniques; 3) experience across multiple industries (ie, data centres and 
electromechanical engineering for the consumer industry) which supports 
better integration; and 4) a complete network, enabling on-the-spot support. 
Acter believes it has the highest market share among the operators of three 
major US data centre players due largely to its capabilities in liquid cooling 
and its expertise in electromechanical and cleanroom engineering.

Catalysts: Near-term: project wins from key clients, supporting stronger 
monthly revenue momentum; mid- to long-term: an improving operating-
margin trend and a resilient order backlog.

Valuation: We are the first non-domestic broker to cover Acter. We initiate 
coverage with a 12-month TP of TWD1,415, based on a target PER of 20x 
(equivalent to a 0.54x PEG over 2026-28E). We believe our valuation is 
fair, given Acter’s strong earnings visibility, which we believe warrants a 
rerating. Our TP is supported by a DCF valuation of TWD1,454.

Risks: 1) weaker-than-expected expansion in the semiconductor industry; 
and 2) project delays or cancellations.

How do we justify our view? 
Growth outlook Valuation Earnings revisions

Growth outlook

Acter: net profit and net-profit growth    
We forecast Acter to record a 36.9% net profit CAGR over 
2026-28E, on the back of a 24.6% revenue CAGR over the 
same period, driven by 1) larger project scale, as strong 
demand for cleanroom EPCM services from the high-tech 
sector is likely to support continued growth in Acter’s order 
backlog and 2) improving gross margins and operating 
efficiency. We expect operating margin to expand by 2.0pp 
in 2027E and 1.3pp in 2028E, driven by favourable gross 
margins on new projects and operating leverage.

Source: Company, Daiwa forecasts

Valuation   Acter: 1-year-forward PER bands    
Our 12-month TP of TWD1,415 is based on a target PER 
of 20x, applied to our 1-year-forward EPS forecast. Our 
target multiple of 20x is higher than the stock’s past-3-year 
1-year-forward PER (12.2x) and past-5-year 1-year-
forward PER (9.6x) and implies a PEG of 0.54x on our 
36.9% net profit CAGR forecast over 2026-28E. We 
believe the stock deserves a rerating in the next 12 
months, largely supported by resilient cleanroom demand 
globally.

Our TP is also supported by an alternative DCF-derived 
valuation of TWD1,454/share.

Source: Bloomberg, Daiwa forecasts

Earnings revisions

Acter: Bloomberg consensus EPS forecast revisions   
Bloomberg consensus EPS estimates for 2026E and 
2027E have risen 24.8% and 24.3% YTD, respectively. 
This is likely due to upward revisions to order backlog and 
earnings forecasts following better-than-expected industry 
outlook. We are the first foreign broker to cover Acter.

Company profile 
Established in 1979, Acter Group (Acter) is a leading engineering services provider specialising in 
cleanroom, electromechanical, and high-tech facility integration solutions for semiconductor, 
optoelectronics, biotech, green energy, and advanced manufacturing clients across Taiwan, China and 
the rest of the world. As of 2025, its revenue comprised cleanroom electromechanical (71%), water, gas 
and chemical delivery systems (20%), consumer industry electromechanical (4%), biomedical (2%) and 
high-tech equipment & materials sales & services (3%).

### Page 5

5

Acter (5536 TT): 7 October 2026 
Acting on the demand for cleanrooms 
Why are cleanrooms a bottleneck in the high-tech 
industry? 
In recent years, cleanrooms have become a critical bottleneck for high-tech industries, 
especially semiconductor manufacturing, OSAT, AI hardware, and PCB, mainly because of 
its indispensable nature, severe supply-demand imbalance, labour shortages, and high 
technical and regulatory barriers.

Cleanrooms are an indispensable core infrastructure as they determine product 
yield and quality stability. Advanced manufacturing nodes in semiconductors, 
optoelectronics, and biopharmaceuticals require highly particle-free, constant-temperature, 
and constant-humidity environments to ensure reliable production. As process 
technologies continue to advance, such as 2nm node (or even newer nodes) and 
advanced packaging, existing cleanrooms must be rebuilt, and the complexity of building 
new ones thus increases. This keeps overall demand for cleanrooms at high levels.

A severe global supply-demand imbalance with demand for cleanroom construction 
outpacing supply. The surge in AI servers, high-performance computing (HPC), and high-
bandwidth memory (HBM) has triggered a global expansion for front-end wafer fabs, 
packaging and testing facilities, and PCB manufacturing. Order visibility remains extremely 
strong across the industry. According to Markets and Markets, the global cleanroom market 
size is set to expand at a CAGR of 6.6% over 2025-31E. At the same time, the global 
semiconductor cleanroom market size is set to grow at a CAGR of 8.0% over 2025-30E. 
For instance, Acter’s order backlog exceeded TWD66bn at the end of July 2026, up from 
TWD46bn at the end of 2025, showing the urgency of demand for cleanroom construction.

Global: cleanroom market size and growth      Global: semiconductor cleanroom market size and growth

Source: Markets and Markets  Source: Markets and Markets

Human resources are an additional limitation to capacity growth. Cleanroom 
engineering is highly technical and labour-intensive, relying on specialised on-site 
installation and supervision that cannot be replaced by robots. At the same time, the 
industry faces severe labour shortages and an aging workforce, and younger generations 
are reluctant to join field operations. Cross-border staff deployment is also less cost-
efficient than local hiring.

Cleanrooms involve complex system integration, creating high barriers to entry. A 
cleanroom construction includes 4 sequential phases, where each phase usually requires 
totally different areas of expertise. Therefore, the competitive landscape in each area 
differs. The process begins with civil & structural (C&S) works to establish the physical 
plant, followed by mechanical, electrical, and plumbing (MEP) works for essential utility 
generation, where Acter is one of the leading global experts. After MEP is finished, 
chemicals/gas/water are then distributed via main systems through primary piping 
network and finally delivered to production equipment through hook-up systems. As

Cleanrooms are a

### Page 6

Acter (5536 TT): 7 October 2026 
global supply chains restructure under N+1 (domestic market + 1) strategies, companies 
building overseas facilities in places like the US face strict and complicated licensing 
processes, particularly on fire safety and hazardous chemicals. Without experience dealing 
with such engineering and safety issues, construction timelines can be severely delayed.

As a result, even with advanced equipment and chip designs, a high-tech company cannot 
begin production without adequate cleanroom space, skilled engineers, and system-
integration capabilities. Therefore, cleanroom engineering contractors hold significant 
bargaining power and capacity allocation leverage in today's tech supply chain.

IC foundry 
In the foundry sector, where the cleanroom supply-demand shortage is the tightest, project 
activity remains centred on advanced process capacity expansion in Taiwan and Arizona. 
Simultaneously, domestic advanced node development remains intense, with multi-phase 
fab builds advancing across Hsinchu, Taichung, and Kaohsiung. Local cleanroom and MEP 
integration demand is further boosted by advanced packaging facilities in Taiwan (such as 
Chiayi and Tainan) and upcoming projects in the US are set to contribute revenue for the 
cleanroom providers in the following years.

Memory 
The memory segment is exhibiting robust momentum as well, mainly catalysed by HBM 
capacity additions. Projects such as the plant in Japan, transitioned to contribution for the 
cleanroom EPCM providers starting from 2026. In Taiwan, turnkey engineering contracts in 
Miaoli are also actively underway. This trend is reinforced by stronger-than-expected 
expansion activity from memory producers in China.

OSAT,  PCB and ODM/OEM 
For OSAT companies, demand is also surging due to the spill-over effect of advanced 
packaging protocols and N+1 supply chain restructuring. The OSAT names are expanding 
their footprint both in Southern Taiwan and in overseas locations including in the US and 
Southeast Asia. Major engineering contracts signed between OSAT clients and Acter show 
stable long-term workload for strong cleanroom demand over our forecast horizon. 
Similarly, overseas expansion for PCB manufacturing is peaking in Southeast Asia, 
especially in Thailand. We expect Chinese PCB players to also increase their capacity, just 
like memory companies in the past year. Except for front-end and back-end chip 
manufacturing, ODM/OEM and data centre infrastructure is also a key growth engine for 
the EPCM industry. Major Taiwanese ODMs are building production facilities in Texas and 
Mexico to meet local manufacturing needs. Cleanroom contractors, such as Acter, 
providing MEP and system integration in Texas are directly benefiting from these projects. 
The shift toward liquid cooling systems has also increased engineering complexity, 
favouring few cleanroom companies with the expertise to deliver complex integration for 
CSPs in both North America and Southeast Asia.

Acter: an integrated cleanroom solutions provider  
Acter Group (Acter), an integrated cleanroom solutions provider, maintains its competitive 
positioning within the high-tech cleanroom and MEP engineering sector, driven by its group 
synergy and global footprint. Acter operates as a comprehensive vendor through a group 
business model. The parent company, Acter, supervises overall cleanroom and MEP 
services, while subsidiary Nova Technology (Novatech, 6613 TT, not rated) mainly provides 
chemical primary system and hook-up, and Novatech’s subsidiary Rayzher (7703 TT, not 
rated) provides gas primary system, hook-up and equipment. In China, Acter operates 
through Acter Technology Integration Group (603163 CH, not rated) and Suzhou Winmax 
(not listed) for Chinese EPCM (engineering, procurement, construction, and management) 
and equipment business. Except for C&S (civil and structural) works, where the margin is 
low (7-8%) and the contract duration is long, Acter’s services span nearly the entire 
cleanroom construction process. Its one-stop solutions model supports customer retention 
Acter provides 
integrated cleanroom 
solutions via various 
subsidiaries

Acter (5536 TT): 7 October 2026 
and improves bid-win rates, particularly for overseas expansion, where customers have 
few reliable providers to choose from.

Acter: organisational chart

Source: Company, Daiwa

To avoid the risk of over-concentration, Acter diversifies its client portfolio across 
semiconductor foundries, memory, PCB, OSAT, ODM hardware assembly, CSP data 
centres and the consumer industry. By sector, semiconductor projects represent the largest 
share of Acter’s portfolio, accounting for 66% of total revenue in 1H26, vs. 69% in 2025. 
Foundry manufacturing is the largest source of demand for cleanrooms in Taiwan, 
especially as advanced-node capacity expands at sites in Hsinchu, Taichung and 
Kaohsiung. In the memory segment, the company executes turnkey engineering builds to 
expand its capacity aggressively, creating cleanroom opportunities across Taiwan, Japan 
and Singapore. This multi-sector coverage decreases the risk of cyclical downturns in any 
individual segment, in our view.

By establishing 33 operating entities across 11 international regions, the company 
effectively captures capex migration driven by global N+1 supply chain diversification. This 
allows Acter to support Taiwanese and international clients as they expand manufacturing 
capacity from Taiwan into Southeast Asia, Japan and the US. Due to the complexity of 
advanced-node IC manufacturing, packaging and testing, it is especially important for 
these Taiwanese semiconductor companies to work with familiar cleanroom builders when 
expanding in the US. This is one of the reasons why Taiwanese cleanroom companies are 
receiving overseas orders and starting to set up global bases, in our view. Despite higher 
management, material, and manpower costs in the US, most cleanroom companies are 
benefitting from the trend by improving margins, given the higher complexity and risks to 
operate overseas. Across overseas hubs in Texas and Phoenix (expected to begin from 
2027), Acter prioritises local hiring and partners with regional civil contractors to shorten 
the learning curve and to avoid the high overhead. To further address the potential labour 
shortages in the future, the company has established cross-border academic and 
vocational partnerships to bring Southeast Asian talent to Taiwan for technical training 
before deploying them back to their home markets, securing future human capacity to 
support global expansion projects.

By product, cleanroom electromechanical services remain Acter’s largest source of 
revenue (accounting for 66% of 1H26 revenue, per the company). These services include 
cleanroom architectural and MEP engineering, which powers airflow, filtration, 
environmental regulation and utility controls. Water, gas and chemical delivery systems are 
its second-largest source of revenue (accounting for 24% of 1H26 revenue, per the 
company). Contributed mainly by Novatech (6613 TT, not rated) and Rayzher (7703 TT, not 
rated), these services cover the main systems and hook-up systems, for  ultra-pure water 
(UPW), specialty gases, chemical piping, etc., all of which require highly precise build-up 
work. Except for cleanroom electromechanical and water, gas and chemical delivery 
systems, the rest of Acter’s revenue comes from the consumer industry services 
electromechanical (5%), biomedical (3%), and high-tech equipment and materials sales 
and services (1%).

Acter: revenue by product

Source: Company, Daiwa

Technically, Acter integrates digital transformation and green engineering into its business 
model, helping it differentiate from peers. Acter deploys digital twin technology and 1:1 3D 
BIM modelling to detect piping interferences and optimise spatial layout before installation, 
minimising schedule delays. This makes Acter a more reliable option, in our view, and thus 
being a favourable supplier, especially for the semiconductor clients that have extremely 
high quality and swiftness criterion.

Bright outlook: strong order visibility 
Rising orders on hand due to supply-demand imbalance for cleanrooms

given the supply-demand imbalance in the cleanroom sector. This is composed of 
semiconductor (63%), electronic assembly (11%), consumer (9%) and others (17%), per 
the company. In 2H25, the company accelerated the digestion of small-scale projects 
below TWD1bn, and, as a result, starting from 1H26, it successfully shifted its project
