(MEP) integration, especially for those data centres that implement liquid-cooling systems. 
The company also wins factory construction projects for Taiwanese ODMs across the US 
and Southeast Asia, as well as PCB facility projects in Thailand and China. Non-tech 
sectors, including biotechnology, commercial buildings, smart logistics hubs and medical 
facilities, comprise 9% of the total backlog, per management.

Acter (5536 TT): 7 October 2026 
Competitive advantages 
High entry barriers to the segment 
Comprehensive product portfolio, empowered by subsidiaries 
Acter provides a one-stop EPCM solution, leveraging its subsidiaries to deliver complete 
solutions. Within the group, the parent company Acter focuses on overall cleanroom and 
MEP system integration; subsidiary Novatech provides primary chemical systems and 
hook-up systems; and sub-subsidiary Rayzer specialises in primary specialty-gas systems 
and hook-up systems. In China, Suzhou Winmax, also a subsidiary of Novatech, focuses 
on the supply of wet processing and chemical handling equipment. This integrated model 
covers cleanroom engineering integration, water, power, chemical and gas-utility systems, 
as well as post-completion validation and maintenance, ensuring cross-system 
compatibility while mitigating risks associated with multi-contractor hiring.

Under global supply chain diversification trends, this unified group strategy strengthens 
order-winning capabilities in overseas markets. Clients expanding internationally face 
regulatory, linguistic and supply-chain complexities, favouring partners that offer one-stop 
services with a proven cross-border track record.

A diverse client base helps Acter weather economic cycles 
Acter’s main competitive advantage is its diversification of clients by industry and region. 
This guides the company through the peaks and troughs of the economy, which is 
especially crucial given the strong cyclicality of the semiconductor/high-tech industry.  Acter 
serves a broad client base across semiconductors, OSAT, PCB, electronics assembly 
(ODM), CSP data centres, biopharmaceuticals and even the consumer industry (including 
commercial real estate and logistics), whereas many peers rely on a few large foundry 
clients. When semiconductor demand slows, capacity expansion in the PCB or data centre 
sectors offset the decline. Additionally, Acter avoids concentration risk and margin 
compression from major foundry accounts, as projects from leading foundries yield lower 
margins; by serving niche markets like US-based CSPs, the company achieves higher 
gross margins.

Geographically, Acter operates across Taiwan, China, Southeast Asia, the US and other 
countries. The company has OSAT and foundry customers in Taiwan and China, PCB 
clients expanding into Thailand, and ODM and data centre clients establishing operations 
in the US. Across different countries, local hiring and local supply chain implementation 
can further reduce costs and increase efficiency relative to peers reliant on a cross-border 
labour force.

Labour shortages 
Amid global labour shortages, Acter minimises the operational impact and turns workforce 
constraints into a moat through localised talent development. Since engineering labour 
cannot easily be automated, Acter has built a cross-border talent pipeline via industry-
academia partnerships. The company recruits Southeast Asian students for specialised 
M&E engineering training in Taiwan, deploying them to global project sites after their 
graduation to solve labour bottlenecks. This structure maintains an engineering team with 
an average tenure of more than 7 years, creating an operational barrier in an industry with 
a high employee turnover rate.

Compared with peers relying on high-cost personnel sent from Taiwan, we note that Acter’s 
local hiring strategy creates cost and efficiency advantages. Prioritising compliant local 
hiring across overseas markets reduces travel expenses while accelerating integration with 
local supply chains and regulations. Localised teams also handle post-completion services 
and technical support directly on-site, building long-term client trust in overseas markets.

### Page 11

Acter (5536 TT): 7 October 2026 
Numerous competitors, each with different specialisation 
Acter faces different competitors across its main product lines. In the EPCM segment, 
including cleanroom and MEP works, key competitors include UIS (2404 TT, not rated), 
L&K (6139 TT, not rated), Yankey (6691 TT, not rated) and Jiuhan (6903 TT, not rated). 
Despite having business models similar to Acter, UIS, Yankey and Jiuhan focus more on 
major semiconductor players in both Taiwan and the US, and L&K concentrates on ASEAN 
semiconductor projects. In the equipment/material sales and agency space, Acter mainly 
competes with Marketech (6196 TT, not rated). In the water, gas and chemical delivery 
systems area, through Novatech and Rayzher, Acter group competes with Marketech, 
Mega Union (6944 TT, not rated), Taiwan Puritic (6826 TT, not rated), Trusval (6667 TT, not 
rated) and Cica-Huntek (6725 TT, not rated).

In our view, despite the competitive environment, Acter maintains a formidable competitive 
moat built on several key advantages. We attribute this to its more than 45 year experience 
in the industry, enabling it to develop specialised expertise that is hard for competitors to 
replicate.

Financial analysis 
We forecast the company’s net profit to rise at a CAGR of 36.9% over 2026-28E, driven by 
a revenue CAGR of 24.6% due to surging demand from the high-tech industry (an increase 
in the order backlog to TWD66bn at the end of July 2026 vs. TWD46bn at end-2025) and a 
3.3pp expansion in operating margin over 2026-28E, driven by the gross margin 
improvement from more favourable project wins and operating leverage.

Strong order visibility, with revenue expected to grow 
across all sectors over 2026-28E 
We forecast Acter’s revenue to increase at a CAGR of 24.6% over 2026-28E, driven by 
scale expansion and a growing demand from various industries, including semiconductor, 
electronic assembly, optoelectronics and others.

Acter: revenue growth trend

Source: Company, Daiwa forecasts

Gross- and operating-margin expansion trend  
Over 2026-28E, we forecast Acter’s gross and operating margins to expand on rising 
revenue scale and a higher complexity of cleanrooms for advanced manufacturing. We 
note that the gross margin from high-tech cleanrooms is higher than that from consumer 
industries, such as commercial buildings and biopharmaceuticals. Thus, we estimate the 
company’s gross margin to reach 26.0% in 2028E from 18.9% in 2025 and its operating 
margin to reach 21.6% in 2028E from 13.7% in 2025.

Acter: gross- and operating-margin trend

(from TWD6,325m in 2026E). We expect its net margin to come in at 13.1% in 2027E and 
14.2% in 2028E (vs. 11.7% in 2026E) and for its net profit to grow at a CAGR of 36.9% 
over 2026-28E.

Intact demand to support revenue growth over our

We forecast Acter’s total cash and equivalents to reach TWD16,838m (36.8% of its total 
assets) in 2026E and increase to TWD27,839m in 2028E (42.8% of total assets) with net

free cash flow (FCF) to improve to TWD12,129m in 2028E, from TWD5,763m in 2026E. 
We believe the capacity expansion cost will initially be low and that the company will 
continue to benefit from the better profitability.

(following steady annual increases to 46.8% in 2026E and 54.9% in 2027E), which we 
attribute to margin expansion from the revenue size increase and gross margin expansion 
led by increases in cleanroom complexity.

Valuation and risks  
Undemanding valuation, alongside its leading global 
positions in the OSAT and memory segments and an 
optimistic industry outlook  
We initiate coverage of Acter with a Buy (1) rating and 12-month TP of TWD1,415, based 
on a target PER of 20x applied to our one-year-forward EPS forecast. This target multiple 
is higher than the average of its past-3-year 1-year-forward PER of 12.2x (ranged from 
7.4x to 23.5x) and the average of its past-5-year 1-year-forward PER of 9.6x (ranged from 
4.4x to 23.5x), which we view as undemanding as we forecast a 36.9% net profit CAGR 
over 2026-28E, implying a 0.54x PEG. This is supported by its business expansion in the 
next 2-3 years. We see potential for a rerating of the stock, given Acter’s global leadership 
in cleanroom EPCM, particularly in the OSAT and memory industries.

The stock is trading currently at a 15.8x 1-year-forward PER vs. its earnings CAGR of 
36.9% over 2026-28E, translating to a 0.43x PEG ratio. Its share price has underperformed 
TAIEX Index by 32.7% in 3Q26, mainly due to its weaker-than-expected 2Q26 results. 
However, given the potential improvement in sector sentiment following TSMC’s results on 
15 October 2026, along with the likelihood that Acter’s revenue momentum will strengthen 
in the coming months, we recommend that investors buy the stock.

We compare Acter with other leading cleanroom EPCM companies such as UIS (2404 TT, 
TWD1,330.0, not rated), L&K (6139 TT, TWD787.0, not rated) and Yankey (6691 TT, 
TWD776.0, not rated). UIS, L&K and Yankey are trading at a PER of 14.7x (earnings 
CAGR of 40.7% over 2025-27E), 9.5x (earnings CAGR of 36.9% over 2026-28E) and 
15.1x (earnings CAGR of 53.6% over 2025-27E), on their 1-year-forward EPS, 
respectively.

Acter’s peers are trading at 18.7x for 2026E and 14.2x for 2027E. We forecast Acter to 
deliver an EPS CAGR of 36.9% over 2026-28E vs. 31.5% CAGR for the segment over the 
same period.

We believe our target one-year-forward PER of 20x (equivalent to a 0.54x PEG using 
2026-28E EPS CAGR) is undemanding as: 1) Acter is ramping up capacity to meet 
growing demand from high-tech customers and is in an expansion cycle; and 2) its 
leading position among OSAT and memory clients warrants a valuation premium, 
compared to its global peers.

Risks to our call  
Primary/key risks: 
Weaker-than-expected expansion in semiconductor industry 
The biggest downside risk to our call for Acter is weaker-than-expected expansion from 
semiconductor clients, especially in advanced-node foundries, advanced packaging and 
OSAT. If global demand for AI servers, HPC or HBM slows, or if clients delay fab build-ups 
and overseas expansion plans, it will directly pressure order inflows and backlog 
conversion for Acter.

Project execution delays or cancellations 
Acter’s revenue recognition is tied to large, complex EPCM and integrated engineering 
projects, which are often subject to customer-driven delays. Any postponement in project 
Our 12-month TP of 
TWD1,415 is also 
supported by our DCF 
valuation

### Page 18

18

Acter (5536 TT): 7 October 2026 
schedule, construction bottlenecks or cancellation of large projects could defer revenue 
recognition and hurt overall profit. This risk is more obvious in overseas projects, where 
licensing, fire-safety approvals and hazardous-chemical regulations are more complicated.

Higher-than-expected raw-material price hikes 
Raw-material costs (especially copper and oil-related costs) account for 50-60% of COGS 
and thus the price volatility makes costs difficult to manage. Acter needs to maintain 
supplier relations, negotiate appropriate price-adjustment mechanisms with clients, and 
invest in R&D to identify composite material substitutes, thereby reducing reliance on any 
single component.

Secondary risks:  
Labour issues or larger-than-expected increases in labour costs  
Cleanroom engineering is highly labour-intensive and relies on on-site installation and 
supervision. Although Acter has built a local hiring and talent-development model, a larger-
than-expected shortage of engineers or rising wage pressure could still raise costs and 
constrain capacity expansion. Any inability to secure enough skilled labour may also limit 
the company’s ability to convert orders into revenue in time.

Margin pressure from lower-margin projects or mix shift 
Acter’s business mix includes a few low-margin C&S services as well as larger EPCM and 
system integration contracts. If the company takes on a higher proportion of lower-margin 
projects, or if competition intensifies in certain geographies or customer segments, gross 
margin could come under pressure. In addition, an unfavourable product mix shift towards 
less profitable work could offset benefits from backlog growth.

Overseas expansion and regulatory risks 
Acter is expanding across markets in the US, Southeast Asia, Japan and China, which 
exposes it to FX movements, local compliance requirements, tax complexity and regulatory 
uncertainty. Delays in licensing, changes in safety standards or local-content requirements 
could increase project costs and reduce execution efficiency. Any misstep in managing 
overseas operations could damage profitability and client retention. 
Key risks to our call: 
weaker-than-expected 
expansion in 
semiconductor industry, 
project delays or 
cancellations, and 
higher-than-expected 
raw-material price hikes

Acter (5536 TT): 7 October 2026 
Appendix 
Company background 
History 
Acter (5536 TT), founded in 1979 and headquartered in Taichung, Taiwan, is a cleanroom 
EPCM provider with over four decades of experience. Starting with early mechanical and 
electrical (M&E) engineering through vertical and horizontal integration, Acter has now 
developed into an engineering technology group with cross-border execution capabilities. A 
cleanroom building process begins with civil & structural (C&S) works to establish the 
physical plant, followed by mechanical, electrical, and plumbing (MEP) works for 
essential utility generation, where Acter is one of the experts. After MEP is finished, 
chemicals/gas/water are then distributed via main systems through primary piping 
networks, and finally delivered to production equipment through hook-up systems. The 
company’s core competency is providing one-stop integrated total solutions, spanning 
services, primary piping networks and hook-up systems, leaving C&S services to other 
engineering peers, given the nature of the latter segment’s low margin and long timespan.

In terms of business model, Acter delivers EPCM (Engineering, Procurement, 
Construction, Management) capabilities. From initial customised planning and feasibility 
assessments to strategic procurement, installation, and post-completion maintenance, 
Acter builds production environments tailored to client process requirements.

Acter: EPCM solutions offered

Source: Company, Daiwa

Regarding global expansion, Acter actively responds to the global supply chain’s “N+1” 
strategy, operating across 11 regions with 33 companies globally. Its service network spans 
Taiwan, China, Southeast Asia (including Thailand, Malaysia, Vietnam, Indonesia, and 
Singapore), Japan, India, and the US. The company leverages a group synergy strategy 
by integrating resources from key subsidiaries – such as Novatech (6613 TT, not rated; 
provides chemical primary and hook-up systems), Rayzher (7703 TT, not rated; provides 
special gas primary and hook-up supply systems), and Acter Group (603163 CH, not rated; 
provides cleanroom engineering in China) – to form a complete technical ecosystem, 
ensuring cross-border capability and quality.

cleanroom EPCM 
provider in Taiwan;

Acter (5536 TT): 7 October 2026 
Acter: global footprint

Source: Company, Daiwa

In 2Q26, Acter classified revenue as: cleanroom electromechanical (63%), water, gas and 
chemical delivery systems (28%), consumer industry electromechanical (6%), biomedical 
(2%) and high-tech equipment & materials sales & services (1%). In 2025, the product mix 
was: cleanroom electromechanical (71%), water, gas and chemical delivery systems 
(20%), consumer industry electromechanical (4%), biomedical (2%) and high-tech 
equipment & materials sales & services (3%).

Acter serves a diversified client base divided into high-tech and consumer divisions. The 
high-tech division focuses on advanced sectors including semiconductors, OSAT, PCB, AI 
data centres (for CSPs), and electronics assembly. The consumer division extends into 
healthcare, premium commercial properties, logistics facilities, and green engineering. This 
multi-industry strategy allows the company to capitalise on tech sector upgrades while 
maintaining resilient operations across technology (especially semiconductor) cycles.

Acter adopts a management strategy involving local hiring in overseas markets, prioritising 
collaboration with local supply chains and compliance with local regulations to reduce 
cross-border deployment costs and thus build strong local teams. Acter has more than 
2,600 employees worldwide (average age: 42 years; average length of service: 7 years). 
With more than 45 years of experience in EPCM, the company plays a reliable role for both 
high-tech and consumer industry.

recycling, and AI smart facility management systems to help clients achieve carbon 
neutrality while expanding capacity. Backed by solid corporate governance, Acter

Glossary of terms 
EPCM (engineering, procurement, construction, and management): Cleanroom 
construction services that cover the whole process including project design, purchasing, 
construction and management.

BIM (building information modelling): A digital modelling system of a building and its key 
information used in cleanroom construction.

C&S (civil & structural): Site infrastructure and the design and construction of load-
bearing elements.

MEP (mechanical, electrical, and plumbing): Building essential systems for environment 
control, power, lighting, water, and drainage.

ESG analysis

Update Date: 7 Oct 2026 
Source:  Daiwa, Company

ESG risks
Risks Management Analyst comments
G
Executive/board
quality 1
Acter maintains a structured governance framework that supports disciplined management. The
chairman is Chin-Li Liang. The company operates with defined responsibility across the board,
management, cyber security oversight, operations, and audit functions. It has established an
organisation function led by the President, a Chief Cyber Security Officer, a Response Team, an
Operations and Policy Enforcement Team, and an Audit Team to supervise policy execution and
review security implementation.
Capital management 1
Acter manages capital in a stable and shareholder-friendly manner. Over the past several years,
Acter has typically maintained a dividend payout ratio of 65-80%. It also maintains a strong
financing network across multiple banks, supporting flexibility for project execution and
expansion while preserving financial stability.
Related party &
transaction 1
Acter’s business is supported by a transparent network of suppliers, project partners, and
financial counterparties, including companies with equity ties and reciprocal business
relationships that are common in its operating ecosystem. These relationships are managed
through formal internal procedures for fund lending, endorsements and guarantees, and asset
acquisition and disposal, all of which are approved through shareholder mechanisms and
company rules. Such a structure helps keep inter-company and supply-chain transactions
organised and traceable, reducing the risk of unclear dealings.
E Product quality &safety 1
Acter executes its projects through a complete, disciplined process covering bidding
assessment, project planning, design, procurement, construction management, verification and
testing, completion and acceptance, closing review, and warranty service. Its cleanroom and
electromechanical engineering business is built around maintaining temperature, humidity, and
dust-free environments that support high-precision manufacturing. The company strengthens
quality control through BIM, Revit, simulation software, ISO drawing output tools, and automated
modeling, which improve design accuracy, construction efficiency, and project consistency.
E Ecological impacts 1
Acter has built a concrete sustainability programme with measurable environmental outcomes.
The company completed Scope 1, 2, and 3 greenhouse gas emissions calculation, obtained
third-party verification under ISO 14064-1:2018, and publicly disclosed energy consumption and
emissions data. It also implemented a strong waste reduction programme, achieving a 95%
wastepaper recycling rate at headquarters in 2025 and 100% paper recycling at the 19F and 20F
floors after establishing paper segregation and reuse procedures. The company also promoted
sustainability measures through emissions inventory management, environmental disclosures,
and resource-efficiency practices. These actions show active environmental management and a
clear commitment to lowering operational impact.
Health & safety

涵蓋區間：台灣時間  10/10 07:50 ⾄  10/13 07:50 （近 72 ⼩時）。本期⼊選 10 件：近 12 ⼩時 6 件、 12 ⾄

事件  1
10/12 克⾥夫蘭聯儲總裁哈瑪克：利率需進⼀步上調（台灣時間  10/13 00:44 ）
五⼤重點
1  2026 年 FOMC （聯邦公開市場委員會）票委、克⾥夫蘭聯儲總裁哈瑪克 10/12 接受專訪並公開
發⾔表⽰，利率需要進⼀步上調，⽬前政策的限制性不⾜，通膨回到 2% 的速度過慢。
2   她傾向「幅度較⼩、頻率較⾼」的升息，但未給出下次升息時點；她並表⽰⾃⼰對中性利率的
估計在 FOMC 中偏⾼，若估錯也只可能是低估。
3   她說明理由：企業計畫漲價幅度超過投⼊成本漲幅，是「通膨思維」固化的早期警訊；能源不
是唯⼀因素，強勁需求也在推升價格。

4   她強調殖利率上升「絕對無法」取代升息，並稱⽬前美債殖利率以歷史標準看屬適中；談話當
天美元指數收漲 0.4% ⾄ 102.58 ，美國現貨公債因哥倫布⽇休市。
5   本週將公布美國 9 ⽉ CPI （消費者物價指數），主席沃什預定本週稍晚發⾔， 10 ⽉ FOMC 會議為
10/27 ⾄ 10/28 ；她的談話是會前靜默期前的重要鷹派訊號。
三⼤結論

### Page 2

1   聯準會 9 ⽉升息⾄ 3.75% ⾄ 4.00% 後，鷹派委員仍在推動下⼀步，市場若把 10 ⽉按兵不動解讀
為升息循環結束，⾵險偏⾼；短天期國庫券仍是承接政策不確定性的⾸選。
2   她點出企業漲價預期與需求⾯通膨，代表就業降溫未必⾜以讓聯準會轉向；當 9 ⽉ CPI 的核⼼
服務與能源同步⾛⾼時，應降低⾼估值成⻑股與⻑天期公債部位，保留短券與現⾦。
3   她強調殖利率上升不能取代升息，意味 10 年期殖利率在 5.2% 附近仍可能被政策路徑推升；當
核⼼ PCE 三個⽉年化維持約 2% 且 10 年期殖利率停⽌創⾼時，才逐步把到期短券轉向⾼品質股
票。

定位，當運輸恢復、庫存回補時應降低部位，不把短期價格衝擊當成⻑期核⼼資產。
2   當布蘭特持續站穩每桶 100 美元以上且美國核⼼服務價格同步上⾏時，應預期聯準會暫停升息
的定價迅速逆轉，並縮短債券存續期間、降低⾼估值成⻑股。

### Page 3

3   能源類股可作通膨對沖，但應優先低成本、⾼⾃由現⾦流與⾼股息的⼤型業者；第四季油價情
境區間為每桶 80 ⾄ 110 美元，若海峽通航恢復，上緣⾵險下降，能源部位應同步調降。

10/12 費城半導體指數跌 2.7% 連四跌，美光跌 3.9% （台灣時間  10/13 04:00 美
股收盤）
五⼤重點
1   費城半導體指數 10/12 下跌 2.7% ，連續第四個交易⽇收⿊，為 7 ⽉以來最⻑連跌；那斯達克跌
0.4% ⾄ 27,245.90 點，道瓊則漲 0.4% ⾄ 51,850.55 點。
2   記憶體股跌幅居前：美光收跌 3.88% ⾄ 989.04 美元，閃迪跌逾 6% ，台積電 ADR 跌 2% 、連五⽇
下跌；同⽇南韓 KOSPI 指數重挫 3.65% ⾄ 6,384.24 點。
3   賣壓主要源於中國⻑鑫存儲的 DRAM 技術進展：其總裁 10/10 表⽰ 4F² 架構研發取得關鍵進展，
預計年底推出整合混合鍵合技術的新⼀代 DDR5 伺服器模組，市場擔憂供給競爭加劇。

4   現貨報價尚未轉弱： DDR5 16Gb 現貨均價 10/12 較前⼀⽇上漲 0.91% ， DDR4 16Gb 上漲
0.29% ，顯⽰這波下跌反映的是競爭預期，⽽⾮報價反轉。
5   光通訊與網通晶⽚同步⾛弱， Lumentum 跌近 10% 、 Semtech 跌 10.15% 、 Marvell 跌 3.42% ，顯
⽰賣壓由記憶體擴散⾄估值較⾼的 AI 硬體族群。
三⼤結論
1   本次下跌的觸發點是競爭者技術進展，⽽⾮記憶體報價或⻑約轉弱；記憶體仍是 AI 供應鏈中議
價⼒最強的環節，但⾼ Beta 個股波動加⼤，應以⿓頭為核⼼、⾼ Beta 為衛星並分批調整。
2   當 DRAM 與 NAND 平均售價先於需求下滑，或⻑約出現減量與延期時，應快速下修記憶體部
位；在報價與位元出貨仍同向成⻑前，股價回檔屬估值重置⽽⾮需求轉折。
3   台積電 ADR 連五⽇下跌，顯⽰資⾦正重新排序⾼估值 AI 硬體；本週台積電與 ASML 第三季財報
是驗證點，當營收、⽑利率與資本⽀出指引同步上修時，才宜回補先進製程與封裝部位。

10/12 美元指數升⾄ 102.58 ，創 2025 年 4 ⽉以來新⾼（台灣時間  10/13 04:00 美
股收盤）
五⼤重點

### Page 4

1   美元指數 10/12 上漲 0.4% 收在 102.58 ，盤中⾼點 102.61 ，為 2025 年 4 ⽉以來最⾼；相對過去 52
週低點 95.55 已回升約 7.4% （推算）。
2   主要貨幣同步⾛弱：歐元兌美元約 1.116 、接近 17 個⽉低點，英鎊滑向 1.32 、接近逾三個⽉低
點，⽇圓在每美元 158.5 附近，逼近曾觸發⽇本當局⼲預的 160 關⼝。
3   推升美元的因素包括中東衝突推⾼油價、市場預期美國通膨再升溫，以及聯準會官員偏鷹發
⾔；美國商品期貨交易委員會持倉資料顯⽰，投機性美元多頭出現近期最⼤的三週增幅。
4   哈薩克央⾏副⾏⻑表⽰，美債殖利率處於多年⾼位，使美元⽐⿈⾦更具吸引⼒，該⾏將把⿈⾦
占外匯儲備⽐重降低約 3 個百分點，反映部分央⾏購⾦需求轉弱。
5   ⿈⾦ 10/12 下跌約 0.6% ⾄每盎司約 4,166 美元，較 52 週⾼點 5,586 美元回落約 25% （推算）；本
週美國 9 ⽉ CPI 若⾼於預期，美元與殖利率可能再⾛⾼。

三⼤結論
1   美元強勢來⾃利差與避險，但⻑線仍受美國逾 40 兆美元債務與⻑債供給牽制，不宜視為單向
交易；保留美元短券作為流動性部位，並依實質利差調節避險⽐率。
2   美元升值提⾼台幣投資⼈的海外資產換匯報酬，也壓抑亞幣與外資流⼊；當美元指數續升且台
韓外資持續流出時，應保留美元現⾦與部分避險，降低未避險的集中曝險。
3   ⿈⾦受美元與實質利率壓制，但財政與地緣尾端⾵險仍在；⿈⾦維持 10% ⾄ 15% 分批避險配
置，當實質利率快速上升時暫緩追價，⽽⾮因短線回落全⾯撤出。

⾼且持續上升、政治與社會環境使財政整頓困難，以及潛在成⻑偏低三項因素拖累。
2   法國 10 年期公債殖利率 10/12 升⾄約 4.9% ，逼近本⽉稍早創下的 5.03% 、逾⼆⼗年⾼點；法德

10 年期利差約 139.5 個基點。
3   預算草案⽬標是把 2027 年⾚字降⾄ GDP 的 5% ，但國會分裂、撙節措施不受歡迎，社會黨與國
⺠聯盟都反對現⾏⽅案，⾚字⽬標的可信度受到質疑。

### Page 5

4   歐元兌美元 10/12 跌破 1.12 、接近 17 個⽉低點，德國 10 年期公債殖利率則⼩幅降⾄ 3.48% ，資
⾦轉向德債避險，顯⽰壓⼒集中在法國⽽⾮全歐元區。
5   後續關鍵時點為國會預算審議與 10/23 穆迪評等檢視；若預算無法在 12/17 前通過，政府可能以
特別法沿⽤舊預算，財政整頓將再延後。
三⼤結論
1   惠譽點名政治環境使整頓困難，代表法國⾵險已從⾚字數字轉向治理能⼒；應降低法國主權債
存續期間與歐元集中曝險，改以跨國低負債、⾼現⾦流企業與短存續期信⽤資產分散。
2   德債殖利率下滑、法德利差維持⾼檔，顯⽰這是單⼀主權⾵險，不應外推為歐元區普遍違約；
當法國預算通過且法德利差收窄時，才考慮提⾼歐洲週期股⽐重。
3   歐元受法國財政拖累， ECB （歐洲央⾏）升息帶來的利差⽀撐被抵銷；在 10/23 穆迪檢視前，
歐股維持衛星配置，以低負債、⾼現⾦流與國防基建受益者為主。

收⼊能否覆蓋新增折舊。

### Page 6

實現收⼊，應以終端變現、 ROIC （投⼊資本報酬率）與⾃由現⾦流作為加碼⾨檻。
2   模型上線並開放 API 與訂閱，代表 Meta 開始把 AI 投⼊轉成可收費產品；當財報顯⽰相關收⼊增
加且⾃由現⾦流未因資本⽀出惡化時，可提⾼雲端與軟體部位，否則維持中性。
3   ⾼利率下股價反應分化，平台公司上漲、上游硬體回檔；配置上維持台積電、先進封裝與液冷
等瓶頸環節為核⼼，對只依賴資本⽀出敘事的標的降低權重。

10/12 中國財政部：新能源⾞出⼝退稅率 2027 年 4 ⽉起由 13% 降⾄ 9% （台灣時
間  10/12 17:08 ）
五⼤重點
1   中國財政部 10/12 公告，⾃ 2027 年 4 ⽉ 1 ⽇起，新能源汽⾞增值稅出⼝退稅率由 13% 下調⾄ 9% ，
並取消電⼦菸裝置等產品的增值稅出⼝退稅。
2   上述產品中徵收消費稅者，出⼝消費稅政策不變、續⽤退（免）稅；適⽤稅率以出⼝報關單註

明的出⼝⽇期認定，⾞廠約有五個多⽉緩衝期。
3   退稅率下降 4 個百分點，等同出⼝⾞每單位貨值多承擔約 4% 的增值稅成本（推算），將壓縮以
價格競爭打⼊海外市場的⾞廠⽑利；今年 1 ⽉中國已公告取消光伏產品出⼝退稅。
4   中汽協 10/12 公布 9 ⽉新能源⾞銷量 182.3 萬輛、年增 13.6% ，占新⾞銷量 61.6% ，連續三個⽉⾼
於 60% ；國內滲透率已⾼，出⼝是⾞廠消化產能的重要管道。
5   新稅率 2027 年 4 ⽉才⽣效，⽣效前可能出現提前出⼝，使 2027 年第⼀季出⼝基期墊⾼，後續解
讀中國汽⾞出⼝數據時需留意這項扭曲。
三⼤結論
1   退稅下調壓縮出⼝⾞廠的價格空間，對依賴出⼝消化產能的中國⾞廠屬利空；中國股市維持低
權重、選擇性配置，不因單⼀產業政策調整改變整體判斷。
2   政策⽅向是減少低價出⼝補貼，短期有助緩和貿易摩擦，但中美休戰只延⾄ 2027 年 1 ⽉ 10 ⽇；
當關稅、稀⼟與 H200 晶⽚交貨出現履約變數時，應進⼀步降低中國出⼝導向部位。

3   中國 8 ⽉社會消費品零售僅年增 0.4% ，⾞市依賴補貼與出⼝；當社會零售、⺠間投資與房地產
資⾦同步回升時，才考慮提⾼中國消費相關部位，在此之前只保留⾼技術製造題材。

施；⽩宮官員隨後說明，這是指雙⽅停⽌攻擊煉油廠的潛在協議，後續可能延伸⾄電廠與電網。

### Page 8

2   ⽩宮官員也承認⽅案不對稱：烏克蘭只有⼀座屢遭攻擊、未完全運轉的煉油廠，俄羅斯則擁有
規模⼤得多的煉油網路。
3   烏克蘭總統澤倫斯基表⽰對川普的說法感到意外，但若俄⽅同意，烏⽅願停⽌攻擊俄國煉油
廠；他 10/12 再指控俄⽅持續攻擊烏克蘭能源設施，基輔當晚發⽣爆炸。
4   消息⼀度壓低油價， 10/12 亞洲早盤 WTI 跌 2.00% ⾄每桶 89.14 美元、布蘭特跌 1.9% ⾄ 100.72 美
元；但中東攻擊隨後升溫，布蘭特當⽇反⽽收漲 1.84% 。
5   俄羅斯副總理諾⽡克 10/12 表⽰，俄國企業正與買家洽談柴油銷售，呼應川普 10/10 所稱俄⽅將
供應⼤量柴油；若落實，可緩解全球柴油供給緊張。
三⼤結論
1   能源停⽕仍停留在宣⽰階段，雙⽅攻擊未停，油價重新由中東供給⾵險主導；原油維持事件避
險，不因停⽕消息提前⼤幅降低部位。
2   若俄烏能源停⽕落實、俄國柴油出⼝恢復，成品油價可望回落，有助通膨在⾼基期後下降；當
運輸恢復且庫存回補時，應降低原油部位。

3   歐洲是能源停⽕最直接的受益者，但法國財政與能源成本仍壓抑估值；當天然氣與柴油價格同
步回落、歐元區通膨連續下降時，才宜提⾼歐洲週期股⽐重。
事件  10
10/10 中國發改委：加⼤逆週期調節，推動重⼤⼯程儘早開⼯（台灣時間  10/10
19:02 ）
五⼤重點
1   中國國家發改委主任鄭柵潔 10/10 主持⺠營企業座談會表⽰，第四季是鞏固經濟回升的關鍵窗
⼝，將加⼤逆週期調節⼒度，強化存量與增量政策協同。
2   他表⽰將推動「⼗五五」規劃與「六張網」建設實施⽅案中明確的重⼤⼯程儘早開⼯，以釋放
內需潛⼒，並推動科技創新與產業創新深度融合。
3   座談企業涵蓋⽯化、天然氣供應、農業機械與 AI 應⽤，議題聚焦第三季經濟形勢與國際供需變
化，反映能源成本與外需波動是當前政策關注重點。
4   消息帶動 10/12 A 股電網設備族群開⾼、⾦冠電氣漲停，但上證指數當天收跌 0.43% 、創業板指
跌 2.61% ，逾 4,300 檔個股下跌，政策利多未扭轉整體賣壓。

### Page 9

5   國家統計局數據顯⽰， 8 ⽉社會消費品零售年增僅 0.4% ， 1 ⾄ 8 ⽉固定資產投資年減 7.2% ，其中
房地產開發投資年減 19.9% ，內需仍是政策要補的缺⼝。
三⼤結論
1   發改委強調逆週期與重⼤⼯程，屬穩投資⽅向，可⽀撐電網與基建相關族群；但⺠間投資與房
地產仍負成⻑，政策效果偏供給側，中國股市維持低權重、選擇性配置。
2   重⼤⼯程若加速開⼯，對銅與電⼒設備需求有⽀撐，並與全球電網升級趨勢相互加成；當現貨
與庫存結構確認供給缺⼝時，可分批持有銅，避免追逐單次政策⾏情。
3   當 9 ⽉社會零售、⺠間投資與房地產資⾦同步改善時，才考慮提⾼中國內需部位；在此之前，
中國資產僅保留⾼技術製造與政策受惠題材。
待確認
1. 推算值：事件 4 美元指數⾃ 52 週低點回升約 7.4% 、⿈⾦較 52 週⾼點回落約 25% ；事件 7 退稅成本約 4% ；事件 8 台積
電兩⽇外資賣超合計逾 2.1 萬張，均為依公開數字⾃⾏推算。
2. 事件 1 ：克⾥夫蘭聯儲官網截⾄本報告未刊出 10/12 講稿，內容以專訪與公開發⾔的即時紀錄為準；美國 9 ⽉ CPI 與

沃什發⾔的確切⽇期待官⽅⾏事曆確認。
3. 事件 2 ：胡塞武裝對機場與阿美設施的攻擊細節為其單⽅宣稱，沙烏地官⽅未逐項證實；超⼤型油輪停航數量為船
舶追蹤資料，屬單⼀來源。
4. 事件 3 ：⻑鑫存儲技術進展被視為賣壓主因，屬市場解讀；閃迪、 Lumentum 、 Semtech 跌幅為收盤報導數字，未
逐⼀以交易所資料核對。
5. 事件 4 ：投機性美元多頭「近期最⼤三週增幅」為單⼀來源整理；⿈⾦價格各來源介於 4,165.80 ⾄ 4,167.56 美元，
報告取約 4,166 美元。
6. 事件 5 ：惠譽評論全⽂未能取得，僅依其公開摘要；法德利差 139.5 個基點為市場資料，屬單⼀來源。

出新指引。

輪油價事件以 10/11 ⾄ 10/12 胡塞攻擊與布蘭特收盤為主體，與上⼀輪「美國財政部制裁 17 艘伊朗相關油輪」（ 10/8 ）

由 90% 提⾼⾄ 99% （ 10/16 ⽣效）並推出外匯穩定措施，屬流動性與匯率操作，⾮法定準備率調整，未單獨成件。

## Document 258: FX Daily_ No trigger in sight for a dollar reversal

Source PDF: all report pdf/FX Daily_ No trigger in sight for a dollar reversal.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-13
Pages: 5
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

A range of indicators suggests the dollar rally is becoming
stretched. But with no clear macro or market catalyst in sight,
we see little reason to call for an imminent reversal. We have
lowered our EUR forecasts to reﬂect a more durable ﬁscal risk
premium, while increasing prospects of a BoE hike should keep
EUR/GBP under pressure for longer

Francesco Pesole, Frantisek Taborsky and Chris Turner
Dollar strength continues to climb, supported by ﬁrmer oil prices
We have published the October edition of FX Talking: Au
revoir, euro bulls, with updated FX forecasts and views.
USD: Overbought signals not that
relevant for now
The start of the week has brought more dollar strength. Firmer oil
prices, following increased military activity in the Gulf, have helped
keep the dollar on solid footing. Today’s calendar is unlikely to be

### Page 2

particularly impactful, with the NFIB survey and home sales ﬁgures
the main highlights.
That said, several technical indicators point to an increasingly
stretched USD rally. Our short-term valuation model shows the
dollar overvalued against every G10 currency except GBP, where it’s
at fair value. On an equally weighted basis, USD is 1.1% overvalued
versus G10. EUR, SEK and NZD stand out as particularly cheap,
undershooting fair value by 1.5-2.5% and sitting beyond the 1.5

standard deviation threshold.
Positioning tells a similar story. Excluding JPY, where long positions
remain unusually elevated, net long USD positioning versus G10
reached 24% of open interest as of last Tuesday. Options markets
are sending the same signal, with 3m DXY-weighted risk reversals
near the peaks seen in March 2025 and March 2026.
Is this enough to call for a reversal in dollar momentum? We do not
think so. In March 2025, it took a Sell-America shock to derail an

overbought USD. In March 2026, expectations of a static Fed
prevented the dollar from beneﬁting from geopolitical tensions. A
clear macro or market catalyst is needed for a reversal, and we do
not see one. Fed tightening expectations remain stickier than those
for most other central banks, limiting USD downside even if oil
prices retreat. At the same time, weakness across global bond
markets continues to support demand for a liquid defensive
currency. Consensus is already looking for a modest 0.2% core CPI

MoM print tomorrow, limiting the scope for a negative USD reaction.
Further ahead, US midterms may represent a somewhat
underappreciated USD risk. However, markets have long priced in
the possibility of Republicans losing control of Congress, and any
precautionary, potentially USD-negative positioning ahead of the
vote may not emerge until late October anyway.
Francesco Pesole
EUR: 1.13 is our new year-end target
EUR/USD is around 1.5% undervalued relative to short-term

fundamentals. This suggests a moderate build-up in France-related

For now, that’s not our baseline. While it’s early to call for a

It’s hardly a welcoming environment for EUR bulls, though. Beyond

hawkish support, while an October move still looks unlikely.

We have lowered our EUR/GBP forecast and now expect the pair to

Idiosyncratic risks surrounding the euro are unlikely to fade
anytime soon, and the Bank of England has sent a clear enough

We still think BoE rate expectations are too hawkish for 2027
(100bp by July), but one or two hikes are looking increasingly likely.
The dovish shift may have to wait until well into 2027,

underpinning GBP strength.
The UK budget on 28 October represents a potential risk for sterling,
as markets are pricing in little ﬁscal concern. Even so, Chancellor
John Healey and Prime Minister Andy Burnham have appeared
mindful of market sensitivities. While the budget will be closely
scrutinised, we do not currently expect it to trigger signiﬁcant bond
volatility or leave a lasting negative mark on GBP.
Francesco Pesole

Romanian inﬂation rose from 6.2% to 6.6% in September,
exceeding market expectations and reversing part of August’s
sharp decline. We and the central bank expect a modest further
increase through year-end before disinﬂation resumes next year.
Czech September inﬂation is expected to be conﬁrmed at 2.5%
later today, shifting attention to the breakdown and core reading.
We estimate core inﬂation edged up from 3.0% to 3.1-3.2%, above
the CNB’s 2.9% forecast. The CNB has long viewed 3.0% as a

potential trigger for rate hikes. With headline inﬂation also above
forecast, we expect the CNB to raise rates again in November.
CEE trading was subdued yesterday amid US market closures,
leaving our outlook largely unchanged. Rising global energy prices
and fresh lows in EUR/USD point to continued pressure on emerging
markets today, supporting our bearish view on CEE currencies. The
Czech koruna remains the exception: a hawkish inﬂation

## Document 317: Shifted the focus back to energy power vendors again

Source PDF: all report pdf/Shifted the focus back to energy power vendors again.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 6
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    陸⾏之  Andrew Lu on global semis and techs <andrewhclu@substack.com>
Subject: Shifted the focus back to energy power vendors again
Date:    9 hours ago
Shifted the focus back to energy power vendors again
View in browser
Shifted the focus back to
energy power vendors again
在⾕歌與 Constellation 簽署兩份⻑期合約後，市場明顯將注意⼒轉
回電⼒能源產業
ANDREW LU ON GLOBAL SEMI/TECHS
OCT 6 ∙ PAID
READ IN APP
What’s New?
Google 與美國最⼤核電業者  Constellation Energy 達成指標性⻑期採購協
議，簽署  20 年期  43 億美元核能⼤單，總供電承諾達  3,590 MW 。

雙軌合約結構（增容  PPA + ⻑期基載電⼒）：
890 MW 功率增容  PPA：為期  20 年， Google 直接資助  Constellation 位
於伊利諾州、賓州與紐澤⻄州的  11 座現有核電機組進⾏翻新與技術升級（更
換蒸汽發⽣器、渦輪機與控制系統），相當於憑空增添⼀座⼤型反應爐的發電
容量，預計  2028 年起陸續供電⾄  2033 年全⾯上線。
2,700 MW 電網電⼒供應協議：為期  15 年，鎖定  PJM 電網內的穩定電⼒配
額（請參考附註了解 PJM 電網架構），為  Constellation 機組提供⻑期現⾦流
保障。
開創「⾃帶電⼒（ Bring Your Own Power, BYOP ）」防禦機制：針對資
料中⼼擴建引發「科技巨頭推⾼⺠⽣電費」的批評，本案採⽤  PJM 電網架
構，由  Google ⾃掏腰包承擔核電站設備升級的巨額資本⽀出，主打不將成本
轉嫁給⼀般居⺠⽤電戶。
科技巨頭核能策略可能轉向「既有設施增容與重啟」：繼微軟重啟三哩島

（ Three Mile Island ）、 Meta 採購  Clinton 核電廠電⼒、亞⾺遜合作  Talen

### Page 2

Energy 後， Google 也從⻑遠的  SMR （⼩型模組化反應爐）進⼀步落實到即
時可⾏的「既有核電廠增容」與「退役電廠重啟」路線。
To read the full post and support our work,
please consider becoming a paid subscriber.
Subscribed
What companies can be beneﬁted and why?
核電與獨⽴發電業者（ Independent Power Producers, IPP ）：
Constellation Energy (CEG)：最直接受益者，獲得確定性極⾼的  43 億
美元投資承諾與  20 年⻑期現⾦流，⼤幅提升估值倍數。（請參考之前深度報
告：What power energy names are beneﬁted more from 100GW
data center expansion? 8 names in comparison ）
Vistra (VST) 、 Talen Energy (TLNE) 、 PSEG：全美⾮受監管核電與基

載電⼒資產被全⾯重估，從過去低成⻑的公⽤事業轉型為  AI 時代具戰略溢價
的稀缺基載資產。
重型發電與核電設備供應商（ Power & Industrial OEMs ）：
GE Vernova (GEV) 、 Westinghouse （ Cameco / Brookﬁeld 持
股）、⻄⾨⼦能源（ Siemens Energy ）：核電增容（ Uprates ）的核⼼在於
更換汽輪機、⼤型變壓器、蒸氣發⽣器與數位監控系統，直接帶動重型電⼒設
備供應鏈龐⼤訂單。
核燃料供應鏈（ Uranium Miners & Enrichment）：
Cameco (CCJ) 、 Centrus Energy (LEU) 、 Uranium Energy (UEC) ：機組
增容運轉及電廠延役延⻑了燃料消耗週期，讓天然鈾（ U3 O8 ）與低濃縮鈾
（ LEU ）的⻑期結構性需求更加穩固。
超⼤規模雲端巨頭（ Google / Alphabet ）：
在  PJM 這個全美資料中⼼最密集且電網負載最吃緊的區域，提前鎖定⻑達  20

年的無碳基載電⼒，避免因電網壅塞⽽被迫延後  AI 伺服器機房上線，同時捍
衛其  2030 年  24/7 零碳排（ CFE ）的承諾。
What companies can be negatively aﬀected and
why?
缺乏儲能的純綠能業者（ Pure-play Wind / Solar Developers ）：

### Page 3

AI 資料中⼼要求  99.999% 的  24/7 恆定供電。純⾵光發電的間歇性在科技巨
頭預算配置中逐漸失寵，科技公司的購電優先順序正轉向核能與地熱，傳統未
配儲能的純光伏 / ⾵電開發商的議價空間遭到壓縮，除⾮再配合儲能系統⼀起打
包出售。
⼆線雲端業者與中⼩規模資料中⼼業者（ Tier-2 CSPs & Mid-size
Colocations ）：
電⼒正取代晶⽚成為算⼒擴張的最⼤瓶頸。巨頭憑藉數⼗億美元級別的資產負
債表包下龐⼤發電量，⼆線廠商在  PJM 等熱⾨電網拿不到⾜夠並網容量，⾯
臨嚴峻的「電⼒排擠效應」。
區域內⾼耗能的傳統製造業（鋼鐵、重化⼯業）：
儘管增容部分的建設由  Google 出資，但⾼達  2,700 MW 的電⼒合約仍鎖定
了電網中龐⼤的供給資源。隨著整體區域備載容量緊縮， PJM 容量拍賣
（ Capacity Auction ）價格⽔漲船⾼，可能進⼀步推⾼當地傳統⼯廠的⽤電
成本。
Our outside-the-box thinking
「核電增容」正在實質截胡  SMR （⼩型模組化反應爐）的⿈⾦窗⼝期：

市場共識普遍認為  SMR 是解決  AI 供電的終極救星，但  SMR ⾯臨審批週期
⻑、⾸台商業化成本過⾼（ FOAK ⾵險）的挑戰。 Constellation 此次透過
11 座現有反應爐增容  890 MW ，直⾔這相當於「在更短時間內憑空建出好幾
座  SMR 」。這意味著在  2028 〜 2035 年間，「增容  + 重啟舊機組」⽬前是科
技業解決⽅案，甚⾄可能使部分商業化進度落後的  SMR 新創公司⾯臨資⾦被
抽離的⽣存危機。
「⾃帶電⼒（ BYOP ）」表⾯上避免推⾼⺠⽣電價，實質上在將公共電網「階
級私有化」：
儘管科技業標榜⾃費升級電廠不影響⼀般⽤電戶，但背後本質是頂級科技資本
以  20 年⻑約包下全美最優質、最穩定的無碳基載發電能⼒。當優質核電被矽
⾕巨頭鎖定，公共電網與⼀般⼤眾將被迫分攤⾼波動、依賴天然氣或間歇性⾵
光的發電⾵險。這在未來數年極可能引發嚴重的監管審查與社會反壟斷反彈
（ Regulatory & Social Backlash ）。
科技巨頭正演變為「影⼦綜合能源公⽤事業（ Shadow Utilities ）」：

Google 這次不是被動向公⽤事業買電，⽽是扮演「電廠重⼤資本改造的出資
⽅兼推⼿」。科技公司的資產負債表已直接穿透⾄發電基礎設施底層。未來衡

### Page 4

量⼀間  AI 超⼤規模雲端巨頭的核⼼壁壘，可能不再單看演算法參數或  GPU 庫
存，⽽是「能源專案融資與物理鎖定基載電⼒的能⼒」。
極端能源溢價將反向倒逼「算⼒能效⾰命」與「端側 / 邊緣  AI 」超預期爆發：
當鎖定幾座資料中⼼的電⼒需動輒砸下  40~50 億美元與  20 年承諾時，雲端
暴⼒堆疊參數量（ Brute-force Scaling ）的邊際經濟效益正在逼近物理極
限。這將逼迫產業加速尋找替代⽅案：更極致的稀疏化模型（ MoE ）、光學計
算晶⽚，以及能將計算負載分散⾄終端設備的「邊緣 / 端側  AI （ Edge AI ）」，
這反⽽可能成為下⼀輪顛覆雲端資料中⼼商業模式的⾮共識催化劑。
附註：什麼是  PJM 電網架構
PJM （ PJM Interconnection ）  是全美規模最⼤、運作機制最成熟的區域輸電
組織（ RTO, Regional Transmission Organization ），負責調度與協調美國
東部及中⻄部  13 個州以及華盛頓特區的⾼壓電網與電⼒批發市場。

其名稱源⾃最初結盟的三個州： Pennsylvania （賓⼣法尼亞）、 Jersey （紐澤
⻄）、 Maryland （⾺⾥蘭）。如今，包含北維吉尼亞州（全球最密集的資料中
⼼聚落  Data Center Alley ）都在  PJM 的管轄範圍內。
⼀、  PJM 的組織⾓⾊與定位
PJM 本⾝不擁有電廠、不擁有電網，也不從事電⼒買賣。它是⼀個受美國聯邦
能源監管委員會（ FERC ）監管的獨⽴⾮營利實體，核⼼⾓⾊有⼆：
電網可靠度調度員（ Grid Reliability Operator ）：控制超過  84,000 英⾥的
⾼壓輸電線路，秒級平衡區域內  6,500 萬⼈的⽤電需求與發電供給。
批發電⼒市場管理者（ Market Operator ）：營運透明、競爭性的電⼒批發市
場，媒合獨⽴發電廠（如核電、燃氣、⾵光發電商）與電⼒零售商 / ⼤⽤電戶。
⼆、  PJM 的三⼤市場架構
PJM 的電⼒市場運作主要依賴三⼤核⼼機制：
電能市場（ Energy Market ）：

分為⽇前市場（ Day-Ahead ）與即時市場（ Real-Time ）。
採⽤分區邊際定價（ LMP, Locational Marginal Pricing ）。 LMP 不僅反映發
電成本，還包含輸電線路壅塞成本（ Congestion ）與線路損耗（ Losses ）。
若某區域（如維吉尼亞機房區）⽤電需求暴增且輸電線路壅塞，當地的  LMP
就會飆⾼。

### Page 5

容量市場（ Capacity Market / RPM, Reliability Pricing Model ）：
這是  PJM 最具特⾊的機制。為確保「未來  3 年」電網在尖峰負載時絕不斷
電， PJM 每年舉⾏容量基礎拍賣（ BRA, Base Residual Auction ）。
發電廠競標承諾在未來特定年份提供⾜夠的備⽤容量，得標者即使不發電，也
能按⽇領取「保證在場費」。近來因⽼舊電廠退役與  AI 負載激增， PJM 容量拍
賣清算價格屢創新⾼。
輔助服務市場（ Ancillary Services Market ）：
採購調頻（ Frequency Regulation ）、備轉容量（ Spinning Reserves ）等
服務，確保電網在發電機跳機或負載突增時，能在數秒⾄數分鐘內維持電網頻
率（ 60Hz ）穩定。
三、  為什麼  AI 巨頭與核電交易都繞不開  PJM ？
當前科技巨頭（微軟、 Google 、 Meta 、亞⾺遜）的核電採購與⼤型資料中⼼
擴建，⾼度集中在  PJM 範圍內，主因在於其獨特的戰略位置與市場結構：

考，並⾮投資建議，個⼈應審慎評估⾵險，如因此造成任何損失，本⼈不負任

Portfolio Strategist

Islamic finance is growing at a rapid pace, driven 
primarily by increasing demand for investors who 
seek Shariah -compliant investments. Sukuk, often 
referred to as Islamic bonds, surpassed USD 1trn in 
2025, making them one of the fastest -growing 
segments o f Islamic finance. The asset class’s 
unique value proposition is driving its popularity, 
especially in an increasingly volatile global  
economic landscape. 
In this publication, we will explore the evolving role of Sukuk in wealth management, 
examining market fundamentals, risk frameworks and emerging opportunities within 
this market.

### Page 3

InvesTips: Sukuk: Structure, stability and strategic opportunity  |  September 2026  3

Sukuk, commonly referred to as Islamic bonds, are Shariah-compliant investment 
certificates representing ownership interests in underlying assets, usufructs (the 
right to use an asset and receive its benefits, without owning it) or ventures. In 
return, Sukuk holders receive periodic distributions, typically in the form of rental 
payments (Ijarah), profit-sharing (Mudarabah) or asset-generated cash flows, and 
upon maturity the principal amount is repaid. Sukuk differ from conventional 
bonds in several ways: 
Fig. 1  
Sukuk vs. conventional bonds

Aspect  Conventional bonds Sukuk

Core principle  Lending (time-value of money) Asset ownership 
Underlying asset  Any asset, business or project Must be Sharia-compliant 
Returns  Guaranteed (fixed or floating interest) Not legally guaranteed (depends on 
underlying asset’s performance) 
Risk   Issuer bears all performance risk Shared risk between parties 
Transparency  Moderate (depends on disclosure) High (asset identification required)

Source: Standard Chartered

The role of Sukuk in the investing landscape is evolving amid 
global uncertainty and rising geopolitical tensions. Investors 
are increasingly seeking more stable and asset ‑backed 
alternatives. An investor can consider investing in Sukuk for 
the following reasons: 
Resilient 
Sukuk provide asset -backed collateral that conventional 
bonds often lack, offering a cushion during volatile market 
conditions. Historically, Islamic investments, including Sukuk’, 
have been countercyclical, i.e. they tend to expand or remain 
stable when c onventional markets contract. Across major 
market stress from the 2018 US-China Trade War through the 
2026 Middle East Conflict, Sukuk bonds consistently 
outperformed Global Bonds — delivering smaller drawdowns 
in risk-off episodes such as the 2018 US-China trade war and 
2022 higher for longer inflation, and greater resilience in 
geopolitical oil shocks, underscoring the asset class's 
structural defensive characteristics.

Strong fundamentals
The Sukuk market experienced an unprecedented 25% growth in issuance in 2025, with issuance nearly USD 300bn. According
to some  reports,  the  Sukuk  market  could  reach  USD  3trn  by  2030  driven  by  increased  demand  for  Shariah-compliant
investments, sovereign funds’ diversification needs, development of regulatory frameworks for Sukuk and growth in Middle East

capital markets.  Nearly  80%  of  Fitch ‑rated Sukuk  carry  investment ‑grade ratings  and  have  little  or  no  history  of  default,
underscoring the sector’s strong fundamentals. These factors should help to instil confidence among investors, who can look at
Sukuk as central building blocks of their portfolio.

Convergence with sustainable investing trends
Sukuk and sustainable bonds share more common ground than their different origins suggest, making sustainable Sukuk a
natural bridge between Islamic finance and sustainable investing. This also helps explain Sukuk’s growing appeal beyond
traditional Muslim investors, as evidenced by Sustainable Sukuk issuance rising from USD 464mn in 2017 to USD 9bn in 2024,

representing a CAGR of 52.7%. The overlap starts with what Sukuk exclude: Shariah principles prohibit interest, excessive
uncertainty and speculation, and screen out sectors financing harmful industries such as tobacco, gambling, weapons, adult
entertainment and alcohol – a negative screen that closely aligns with the exclusionary (negative screening) method, commonly
used in sustainable investing. Tradi tional Sukuk bonds require proceeds to be linked to identifiable real assets or projects,

creating a ring-fenced structure like use-of-proceeds sustainable bonds. Combined with Shariah’s broader objectives of social
welfare, resource stewardship and equita ble risk-sharing, this creates strong alignment with the United Nations Sustainable
Development Goals.
Green Sukuk is an established example, where proceeds are earmarked for environmental projects such as renewable energy,
clean transport, energy-efficient buildings or sustainable water and wastewater management. Issuers must meet both Shariah

approval from a recognised scholarly board and alignment with International Capital Market Association (ICMA) Green Bond
Principles.
Traditional Sukuk already comply with Shariah laws such as avoiding Riba (interest -based returns arising from lending
transactions), Gharar (investments with ambiguity) and Maysir (speculation). Sustainable Sukuk build on this foundation by
adding environmental and social objectives. The table below highlights how sustainable Sukuk differ from traditional Sukuk.

Fig. 4
Traditional Sukuk vs. Sustainable bonds vs Sustainable Sukuk

Digital integration
The integration  of  blockchain  technology  and  digital  platforms  is  revolutionising  Sukuk  issuance  and  trading,  dramatically
reducing transaction costs and expanding accessibility to  a broader  investor base.  For instance, SAKKEX (an  independent
blockchain-based Sukuk marketplace) streamlines drafting, issuance, and near ‑instant settlement. Its Sukuk Wallet App also
allows investors to trade on the go. Several such digital platforms now allow tokenised Sukuk that enable fractional ownership,

automated Shariah  compliance  verification  and  real-time  settlement,  thereby  addressing  challenges  that  have  persisted  in
Islamic finance markets. Digital tools also simplify cross border Sukuk transactions by enabling end to end digital origination and
trading, with smart contracts automating profit sharing to cut operational costs and enhance transparency.

### Page 6

InvesTips: Sukuk: Structure, stability and strategic opportunity  |  September 2026 6
Sukuk structures define the nature of ownership, risk -sharing and return
generation. Several types of Sukuk structures exist and are defined by the
underlying Shariah contract. We discuss some prominent ones below and see
how they align with various phases of an investor’s wealth journey:
Sukuk al-Ijarah is a Shariah-compliant lease-based bond where the investor gains partial ownership in a physical

asset such as a building, airport or equipment. The asset is then leased back to the issuer who pays a nominal
(pre-agreed) rent to the investor. An investor seeking stable, predictable income streams with lower volatility can
consider investing in these Sukuk. The original principal amount is returned at the end of the lease period.
Ijarah Sukuk are ideal for the wealth preservation phase of an investor’s wealth journey. For instance, investors
nearing retirement typically need to restructure their portfolios by shifting from growth to preservation while

generating steady income to cover living expenses. Ijarah Sukuk deliver on both fronts: protecting capital through
asset-backed structures while providing predictable rental income that supports household needs without
depleting principal.
Sukuk al-Murabahah are cost-plus financing structures where the issuer buys an asset and immediately resells
it to the investors at a marked-up price, with payment deferred over a pre-determined time. Consider this: Let us

assume, a company needs USD 100m. A special purpose vehicle (SPV), established by the company, mobilises
funds from investors and buys commodities worth USD 100m and immediately resells them to the company for
USD 112m payable over time. The USD 12m difference is the agreed profit margin. Murabahah Sukuk ar e best
suited for investors seeking income over a short to medium term, typically, 1-5 years. Investments in this type of
Sukuk return the principal gradually throughout the investment period rather than only at maturity.

Investments in Murabahah Sukuk are best suited for wealth protection and income generation. Unlike Ijarah which
return principal as a lump sum at maturity, Murabahah amortise both profit and principal in each instalment. This
makes them ideal for retirees who need to systematically draw down capital to fund living expenses without
depleting their portfolio too quickly.
Sukuk al-Mudarabah is a profit-sharing partnership arrangement where investors provide 100% of the capital

and operate as silent partners. The issuer manages the business or project as the active partner. Under a
Mudarabah arrangement, financial losses arising from normal bu siness risks are borne by the investors in
proportion to their capital contribution, while the Mudarib loses its effort and entitlement to profit. However, where
losses result from negligence, misconduct, breach of agreed terms, or misconduct by the Mudari b, the Mudarib
may be held liable. Think of it as venture capital meets bonds; you fund the business, professionals run it and

you share the profits or bear the losses resulting from the business activities. Mudarabah Sukuk are often
regarded as one of the  structures most closely aligned with the Shariah principle of profit and loss sharing.
However, they bear higher risk and lower predictability compared to other structures.
Investors with a growth mindset and open to high -risk tolerance can consider investing in these Sukuk . They
best align with accumulation and growth phase of the wealth journey, typically, when investors have long time

horizons to absorb volatility and can afford capital risk for higher returns. However, to balance risk, it is important
for investors to diversify across various types of Sukuk. Even within Mudarabah, they can consider investing
in multiple projects, sectors and geographies.
1 Sukuk structures and
wealth mandates

### Page 7

InvesTips: Sukuk: Structure, stability and strategic opportunity  |  September 2026  7

A joint venture partnership structure where both investors and the issuer contribute capital is Sukuk al -
Musharakah. In this structure, both parties (issuer and investors) jointly provide capital, participate in managing 
the venture, and share profits or losses based on agreed upon ratios. This structure appeals to investors focused 
on growth, interested in active participation, and prepared to take on greater risk in pursuit of higher returns. 
Musharakah Sukuk are best suited for the growth and accumulation phase where investors are building wealth 
aggressively, comfortable with high risk and able to lock up capital for the long term (typically 5+ years).

Sukuk al-Wakalah is an agency arrangement where investors appoint a representative from the issuing company 
as their Wakil (agent) to invest their funds in specific Shariah-compliant assets or projects in exchange for a fee. 
This arrangement is similar to a mutual fund or ETF, where the agent manages a diversified pool of Shariah 
compliant assets on behalf of the investors. Investors benefit from professional fund management, diversification, 
transparency and flexibility. Depending on issuer preferences, Wakalah Sukuk can b e structured with lower 
investment thresholds, making them accessible to a wide range of investors. 
Wakalah Sukuk suitability depends heavily on the underlying asset allocation and risk profile. Conservative 
Wakalah structures investing in stable, income -generating assets suit wealth preservation. Moderate -risk 
Wakalah can complement accumulation portfolios as a diversification tool, though not as a primary growth driver 
as their structure and risk mandate limit upside potential.

Sukuk al-Istisna is a project financing structure based on a manufacturing or construction contract. Investors 
provide upfront capital to finance the construction of specific assets. Upon completion, the asset is sold to an end 
buyer (often the original issuer or a third party) who makes deferred payments including the cost plus a mark-up, 
which are then distributed to investors. Istisna suit seasoned investors with construction finance expertise and 
those who can absorb development risk and commit capital for the long term. Owing to its complexity and higher 
risk profile, this type of Sukuk typically requires large initial investments, making it less accessible to retail 
investors.  
In the wealth cycle, Istisna are best aligned with the accumulation and growth phase, when long time horizons 
can absorb construction delays and development risks. The long-term commitment and potential for cost overruns 
or project failure make it unsuitable for later wealth stages such as retirement.

Sukuk al -Salam is an Islamic bond backed by a forward purchase contract where investors pay upfront for 
commodities to be delivered later. At its core, they are similar to forward contracts. However, unlike a forward 
contract, Salam contracts are fully prepaid, standardi sed and designed with clearly defined delivery terms to 
reduce uncertainty (gharar). Under this arrangement, an investor profits from the difference between the lower 
Salam purchase price (paid today) and the higher resale price (received at delivery).  
Salam Sukuk carry high delivery and price risk and aligns well with investors in the growth and accumulation 
phase. Salam requires strong commodity‑market expertise, deep understanding of production and delivery risks, 
and the ability to assess the issuer’s operational capacity to deliver the specified goods on time.

Owing to their ethical framework and asset -backed nature, Sukuk are often
considered relatively less risky than conventional bonds. However, they are not
immune to risks. Unique challenges such as asset quality deterioration and
structure-specific exposures require rigorous risk management.

Source: Standard Chartered
Due diligence in Sukuk extends beyond traditional credit
analysis and an investor should also consider the following:
• Shariah compliance verification can be done by evaluating

the Sukuk structure and confirming that its mandates and
underlying assets fully align with Islamic principles.
• Evaluating asset quality by verifying tangibility, cash -
generating capability and legal ownership can help
investor reduce risk.
• Assessing creditworthiness and market depth will help in
identifying financially strong Sukuk issuers and ensuring
adequate liquidity.
• Cross-border Sukuk investments remain viable,
contingent on careful scrutiny of legal documentation

across multiple jurisdictions to ensure enforceability and
investor protection.
Robust due diligence and proactive risk mitigation strategies
are essential to protect capital and stay aligned with Islamic
financial principles.
1 Risk management and due diligence

### Page 10

InvesTips: Sukuk: Structure, stability and strategic opportunity  |  September 2026  10

Staying up to date on the latest developments in 
the Sukuk market is important as this space is 
constantly evolving and rapidly expanding. 
Moreover, Islamic finance is not static; Shariah 
interpretations related to Sukuk structures are 
periodically redefined. Staying informed protects 
investors from investing in non -compliant 
options. We look at some of the factors that are 
guiding the future of this landscape:

1 Emerging trends

Green Sukuk issuance is projected to grow materially through 
2030, reflecting the natural convergence of Islamic finance and 
sustainability principles. Key sectors include renewable 
energy, green buildings, low -carbon transportation, water and 
waste management and sustainable agriculture.

Tech advancements  are revolutionising Sukuk origination, 
trading and accessibility. Digitisation offers numerous benefits 
to the Islamic finance landscape: streamlined issuance and 
trading processes, enhanced accessibility through 
tokenisation, immutable audit trails via bl ockchain technology 
and improved secondary market liquidity.

A major regulatory shift is likely to create standardised rules 
across Islamic finance markets globally. The pivotal 
development is AAOIFI's Standard 62, launching in 2026, 
which mandates transparent, Shariah-compliant asset-backing 
and genuine asset ownership. This can fundament ally 
strengthen the Islamic authenticity of Sukuk structures.

The Sukuk market is rapidly expanding beyond its traditional 
Islamic finance strongholds. Non-traditional territories  in 
Africa (e.g., Algeria, Benin and Nigeria), Europe (e.g., 
Luxemburg, UK) and Asia (e.g., Turkey, Pakistan) are 
emerging as Islamic capital market gateways. This expansion 
underscores the evolution of Sukuk from a niche Islamic 
product to a globally reco gnised asset class attracting diverse 
sovereigns and investors across continents.

### Page 11

InvesTips: Sukuk: Structure, stability and strategic opportunity  |  September 2026  11

Conclusion

Ultimately, Sukuk demonstrate that Shariah compliance and sound investment 
strategy are not competing priorities, but complementary ones. By ensuring that 
portfolios are grounded in real economic activity by offering stability through 
tangible asset -backing and transparent structures that channel capital towards 
productive use. Their foundation in ethical, responsible financing appeals to 
investors seeking both resilience and purpose. As global markets increasingly 
prioritise integrity and long-term sustainability, Sukuk stand out as a compelling tool 
for Shariah -compliant investors who want meaningful diversification without 
compromising on their religious and ethical principles.

## Document 330: The Institute Employment Report_ September 2026 Bank of America Institute analysis shows slower payroll growth, declining unemployment payments and continued strength in lower-inco

Source PDF: all report pdf/The Institute Employment Report_ September 2026 Bank of America Institute analysis shows slower payroll growth, declining unemployment payments and continued strength in lower-inco_223e3a6a.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 5
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

13025075     1

Economy 
The Institute Employment Report: September 2026 
07 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Job growth softened further in September, according to Bank of America's deposit data, with growth of 1.4% year-over-year 
(YoY), from 1.5% in August. But falling unemployment payments in September indicate a relatively healthy overall picture. 
• Lower-income households' wage growth remains strongest, despite a narrowing gap. After-tax wage growth eased to 4.5% YoY 
for lower-income households in September, while rising to 3.8% and 3.7% for middle- and higher-income households, 
respectively. 
• Lower tax withholdings and a relative strengthening in the younger/lower-income end of the labor market are driving this story. 
Additionally, average hours have risen in some lower-paid industries, which is also boosting pay packets.

Job growth slowed in September, but unemployment trends are benign  
Our estimate of job growth – which uses Bank of America deposit data to track changes in the number of customer accounts 
receiving a paycheck (see Methodology) – slowed further in September, to 1.4% year-over-year (YoY) from 1.5% YoY in August. 
The data can be noisy, partly due to seasonal variation and differences in pay-period timing, but in our view, the latest reading is 
consistent with the easing in payrolls growth seen in the September Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) data (Exhibit 1). 
Still, despite slower hiring, there is little sign of a rise in joblessness in Bank of America data. Unemployment payments into 
customer accounts showed a YoY decline in September (Exhibit 2). So, overall, labor market trends appear resilient.

After-tax wage growth cooled for lower-income, but rose for other cohorts 
Lower-income wage growth eased to 4.5% YoY in September from 4.7% YoY in August, while wage growth for middle- and 
higher-income households rose to 3.8% and 3.7% YoY, respectively. Overall, these changes were modest, and lower-income 
households are continuing to see faster after-tax wage growth than other cohorts, a notable turnaround from the “K-shaped” 
pattern we observed over much of 2025 and 2026 (Exhibit 3). 
Exhibit 3: Lower-income wage growth remains strongest, despite a narrowing gap 
After-tax wage and salary growth by household income terciles, based on Bank of America aggregated consumer deposit account data (three-month moving 
average, YoY%, SA)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
In our view, the pick-up in lower- and middle-income households’ after-tax wage growth continues to reflect the impact of the 
One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBA) in reducing tax withholdings, alongside an underlying rise in job-to-job changes at the 
younger/lower-income end of the labor market (for more, read: The Institute Employment Report: August 2026). Consistent with 
that underlying pick up, BLS data shows a solid YoY rise in average hours worked in several relatively lower-paid industries, such 
as manufacturing, retail and other services. Hourly workers in these industries are likely seeing rising paychecks as a result 
(Exhibit 4).

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Estimate of payroll growth from Bank of America internal data is based on the change in customer accounts receiving a paycheck in the month. 
An adjustment is made for the difference between overall population growth and customer account growth. Occasionally historic payments can 
be reclassified, leading to revisions in the series.  
An estimate of bonus growth from Bank of America deposit data is calculated by looking at customers who have received an inbound ACH 
payroll transaction in the last two years. From this sample an estimate of bonuses is derived by looking for payroll transactions which are over 
50% higher than the median regular payroll payments received by the customer. Of these payments only those that were received around the 
same time in each of the last two years are selected.  
The job-to-job change rate (j2j rate) is defined as the proportion of customers with an identified change in their employer as a proportion of 
the total number of customers with employment income. We estimate the median pay rise associated with a j2j change using the pay in the 
first three months of the new job compared to the same three months a year ago. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
• The Westpac–DataX Card Tracker
Index* has edged up slightly since late
August, rising 0.2pts to 159.1.
• Quarterly growth momentum has
seen a further lift and is now tracking
towards a 1.7% gain for the September
quarter which would mark the strongest

quarterly rise since June 2025.
• However, both the monthly and weekly
reads show momentum has faded over
the course of the quarter. After posting
0.7-0.9%mth gains through May, June
and July, the Index rose just 0.2%
in August and is tracking a similarly
subdued 0.3% pace for September.
Weekly reads are also tracking a more
moderate trend pace of 0.2%mth.
• On a quarterly basis, the detail shows
discretionary services and international
transactions have led the pick-up.

While some of this looks to be catch-
up from travel disruptions in the June
quarter, gains have been consistent and
broadly based. Likewise, there is little
evidence that the pick-up is coming from
a lift in prices with recent CPI updates
suggesting services inflation has been
steady rather than accelerating (see
here).
• On the apparent loss of momentum
over the course of the quarter, the detail
suggests this is a re-run of the fuel price
squeeze in March-April-May with the

essentials spend gain rising strongly. So
far the spillover effects are showing up
mainly as a dampening in discretionary
goods spend with the slowing in
discretionary services largely confined to
hospitality.
• Overall, the card tracker data suggests
the Q3 spending data will still come in
as a relatively solid gain, even allowing
for a firmer gain in prices in the quarter.
However, the fade in momentum is
notable with the impact of further fuel

pricer increases and this week’s RBA rate
rise still to come. Add what now looks
more likely to be a follow-on interest
rate hike in November and the December
quarter is shaping as a significant
slowdown in card activity.
“... both the monthly
and weekly reads show
momentum has faded over
the course of the quarter.”

WESTPAC ECONOMICS 9
About the Westpac card data indicators
The indicators presented in this report are based on the millions of credit and debit card
transactions processed every day. Transactions covering over ten million merchants are
classified into over 700 categories. These are in turn grouped into higher level aggregates

that provide a timely guide to wider economic trends.
The main focus of these indicators is consumer spending. Where possible, we have sought
to exclude ‘non spending’ transactions such as: money transfers; tax payments; loan
repayments; charitable donations; and superannuation contributions.
It should also be noted that these indicators will also be affected by shifts between card and
non-card transactions. This was a significant factor during the COVID–19 pandemic – health

concerns about the use of physical cash leading to significantly higher use of cards vs cash,
particularly where contact–less transactions are available. Transaction flows also include
reversals/refunds which were also a significant phenomenon during the onset of COVID,
especially in areas such as travel.
All transaction data is compiled at a highly aggregated level so that individual customer or
merchant data is never revealed.
Index construction

The key metrics used in this report are indexes of spending–related card activity where the
base of 100 is average activity in 2019. As an example, if transaction flows are 5% above their
average level in 2019, the index read for the period is 105. If flows in a subsequent period are
8% above the average level in 2019, the index read for this period is 108. Growth between the
two periods can be calculated simply as the change between the two index reads, i.e. 2.9%.

All measures are adjusted for regular seasonality. Weekly estimates are generated using
the US Bureau of Labor’s MoveReg weekly seasonal adjustment program. Note that in some
cases, high levels of volatility during the COVID period mean it is not possible to produce
seasonally adjusted estimates for some historical periods.
Also, note that previous versions of this report used different approaches to seasonal
adjustment and measurement more generally. This means Index reads are sometimes not

directly comparable. See the ‘About the Westpac card data indicators’ sections from earlier
reports to more detail.
Classifications
Note that the measures used for card data and in this report do not align completely with
the those used in official ABS statistics, including the ABS household spending indicator,
ABS retail trade survey and ABS estimate of consumer spending published in the national
accounts. There are a range of differences including around both coverage and classification.

As such, the card data should be treated as broadly indicative.
The transaction data is grouped into 26 categories that are then combined into four main as
follows:
Discretionary goods: alcoholic beverages, tobacco, clothing & footwear, furnishings &
household equipment, household appliances, vehicle–related, recreational & cultural goods,
newspaper, books & stationery, and other personal effects.
Discretionary services: transport services (part), recreational & cultural services, gambling,

catering services, accommodation services, other personal care, insurance & financial
services, other services.
Essential goods: food & non alcoholic beverages, medical products, appliances & equipment,
and operation of personal transport equipment.
Essential services: electricity, gas & other fuels, health services, transport services (part),
communications and education.
The report also uses two additional classifications:
MHSI/Retail: based on the extent to which categories cover activity that is in scope for the

ABS monthly household spending indicator and ABS retail trade survey.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS  
NOVEMBER CPI
PREVIEW AND ANALYSIS
Complete Montly CPI will take some time to understand
19 December 2025
Key points
• The October monthly CPI was 
flat in the month, broadly as we 
expected but stronger than market 
expectations. For more information see 
“Electrical noise is far from static”.
• The impact on our inflation profile 
was marginal. The December quarter 
CPI remains 0.6%qtr/3.6%yr, while 
the Trimmed Mean edged up from 
0.7%qtr/3.1%yr to 0.8%qtr/3.2%yr.
• Our November monthly CPI estimate 
is 0.4% for the month, leaving the 
annual pace unchanged at 3.8%yr.
• The introduction of the complete 
monthly CPI is a significant enhancement 
to our analysis of price formation in 
the Australian economy. However, 
with many series having only a short 
history, it will take time to fully 
understand some of the finer details.
• For now, we will continue to place 
greater emphasis on the quarterly 
measures of core inflation.

### Page 2

Past performance is not a reliable indicator of future performance. The forecasts given above are predictive in character. Whilst every effort has been taken to ensure that the assumptions on which the forecasts

2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
No surprise in the October CPI
The new complete monthly CPI gained 3.8% in the year to 
October, coming in a touch softer than Westpac’s estimate of 
3.9%yr but a bit stronger than the market estimate of 3.6%yr. 
So while the market and other commentators may have been 
surprised by the result it was broadly within our expectations 
and as such has had just a marginal impact on our inflation 
profile.
The October CPI was flat in the month, a touch softer than 
Westpac’s published near-cast of 0.1% on the back of a larger 
than expected decline in electricity (–10.2%) which was only 
partially offset by a smaller than expected decline in auto fuel 
(–0.9%) and an unexpected bump up in garments (+2.6%).
The Trimmed Mean (TM) lifted 0.3% in the month of October, 
the same monthly increase it has seen for the previous two 
months and down from the 0.5%mth increase in July but 
stronger than the 0.2%mth prints from March to June.
This saw the annual pace of the TM increase to 3.3% from 
3.2% in September. This month the TM excluded the annual 
rise in electricity; alongside other large price rises and falls. 
Due to the short history of many series in the monthly CPI, the 
annual pace of monthly TM inflation can only be calculated 
back to April 2025. Before then the ABS notes that the annual 
pace is calculated from the quarterly CPI data.
For more information on the October monthly CPI see 
“Electrical noise is far from static ”.
Monthly CPI points to some momentum
Following the October CPI we have seen some minor revisions 
to the various components in our December quarter CPI 
estimate with the upward revisions, such as transport via auto 
fuel and motor vehicles, being offset by downward revisions 
to recreation, alcohol & tobacco and utilities in particular. This 
has left our December CPI estimate unchanged at 0.6%qtr.   
As our Chief Economist, Luci Ellis, has noted that inflation 
did see a bump in the September quarter and again in the 
month of October. However, the main sources of the surprise 
have had little to do with domestic demand or labour market 
pressures. Rather, a sizeable part of the bump looks to have 
been from administered prices and noise. Since the release of 
the data, the RBA has recognised this issue but at the post 
RBA Board Decision Media Conference in December, the 
Governor communicated that the Bank is more worried about 
upside risks to inflation than first thought.
We agree that some of this momentum will continue into the 
December quarter as highlighted by our upward revision in our 
We will continue to focus on the quarterly prints
Justin Smirk
Senior Economist

BULLETIN
December quarter TM estimate to 0.8%qtr/3.2%yr from our 
earlier estimate of 0.7%qtr/3.1%yr. 
For the December quarter, price changes that are included in 
the TM go from a low of –0.26% to a high of 1.66%. Readers 
will remember that the TM is calculated from seasonally 
adjusted price changes and not the original estimates so if 
unaffected by regular seasonal price shifts. We estimate 
that the most significant components to be trimmed off 
the top in the quarter are: electricity (2.7%); other services 
for motor vehicles (2.5%); tobacco (2.1%); and automotive 
fuel (1.7%). Trimmed off the bottom are: other non-durable 
household products (–0.39%); garments for women (–0.92%); 
international holiday travel (–0.97%); vegetables (–1.16%) and 
wine (–1.27%).   
However, we do not expect this momentum to continue 
through 2026. As Luci noted in her article “ RBA on hold 
in 2026, risks on both sides ” we expect inflation to keep 
easing, due to the lagged impact of past restrictive policy 
and the unwind of earlier government support measures. We 
believe that many commentators have overlooked these lags. 
Monetary policy typically takes a year or more to fully affect 
jobs and prices, so current outcomes reflect the peak cash rate 
from a year ago and we expect to see further labour market 
softening. Strong hiring across the care economy has masked 
some of that restrictiveness, as has public infrastructure 
spending, but these supports are now fading along with last 
year’s tax-cut boost. We believe that even with the 75bps in  
rate cuts this year, policy remains mildly restrictive, and given 
usual lags, this will keep showing in the data through 2026.
Taking a look at some of the recent larger moves in monthly 
prices can highlight many of the issues Luci raises. Taking the 
seasonally adjusted data, smoothing it with a three month 
average then calculating the change over three months (so we 
have three months on three months percentage change) we 
find at the top end of the list some price increases you would 
not expect to continue at the same pace. 
For example at the top are: lamb & goat (8.1%, highly 
seasonal); accessories (8.1% with jewellery prices surging 
on the back of record high gold prices); audio visual & 
computing services (6.0% due to Netflix subscriptions); beef 
& veal (3.7%, highly seasonal); coffee & tea (3.6% noting 
global coffee prices have passed their peak and are already 
significantly lower); water & sewerage (2.3% IPART decision 
was for a 13.8% increase for Sydney Water in October); 
property rates & charges (2.3% administered prices); and 
electricity (1.2%, reflecting unwinding of cost of living 
rebates). Offsetting some of these increases was falling prices 
for: men’s footwear (–2.0%); vegetables (–2.0% seasonal); 
eggs (–1.5%, reflecting the unwinding of the price spike 
associated with an outbreak of bird flu); and major household 
appliances (–1.2%). 
Core inflation still to moderate through 2026
We are projecting headline inflation to get back to the RBA’s 
target band, but not until later in 2026. We now see core 
inflation back below the midpoint of the 2–3% range at the 
end of the year. We had thought this might be achieved by the 
third quarter but now expect current momentum to continue

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
for a bit longer. However, it is worth noting that our estimate
of the six month annualised pace of the TM is down to 2.4%yr
by June 2026.
Electricity to boost November CPI
Westpac is estimating a 0.4% increase in the November

monthly CPI which will hold the annual pace flat at 3.8%yr.
The seasonally adjusted estimate is 0.5% in the month
suggesting it is a stronger than usual estimate.
Boosting the November estimate is: a 16% surge in electricity
(as the impact of the last round of rebates fades); a 3.2% jump
in gas & other household fuels; and a 2.3% bump in auto fuel
(based on reported pump prices). Partially offsetting these
gains is a –2.6% decline in holiday travel & accommodation

and a –2.9% drop in clothing & footwear.
Take care with the Monthly CPI
Many series in the monthly CPI only date back to early 2024
so seasonal adjustment is somewhat problematic. Some
series did have a longer monthly history coming from the
previously published monthly CPI indicator and it appears
the ABS may have used some of this to seasonally adjust the
data. However, we caution that some of the new data has a
different history to the old data and as such, we expect it is

going to take some time to understand the seasonal behaviour
of the new data.
For example, for male garments there is some difference
with the reported monthly change compared to the old CPI
Indicator series but this appears to be only marginal. However,
for women’s, the difference can be significant with even a
reversal of the sign of change in some recent months. And for
some series with a short history and no monthly indicator data
to compare to some of the adjustment appears to be a little

arbitrary, as you can see in the chart right which compares the
children’s garments index in original and seasonally adjusted
terms. The only adjustment made to the series was in June
2025 when a –3.2% change was adjusted to a flat print. Why?
We are yet to uncover.
You can also see where the ABS has turned to smoothing
processes to seasonally adjust short data series. In the chart
over the seasonally adjusted series looks as if has been
generated by applying a Henderson Trend to the original

children’s footwear series.
As such, we expect it is possible there will be significant
adjustments made to the seasonally adjustment process as
data is collected which could result in significant revisions to
seasonally adjusted series including the Trimmed Mean and
the Weighted Median.
